03Reading tickets and modificationsThree total, not five+

Your goal

Distinguish a replacement ticket from an added order and keep a one-item substitution linked to the current quantity.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Three total, not five

Ticket 41 originally requests two tarts. A new slip says "three tarts, revised 41." Cook Imani asks expediter Cole whether the new slip replaces or adds to the old quantity. Cole confirms that it replaces it: the total is three tarts, not five. One of the three has salad instead of potatoes. The conversation does not establish a seat number, cooking completion, or allergy. Imani must retain the one-item substitution, clarify the actual item identification through the ticket process, and prevent both versions from being treated as separate orders.

Imani: Cook on the tart station / Cole: Expediter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the current total for ticket 41?

Answer and explanation

D. Three tarts

Cole confirms that the revised quantity replaces the earlier quantity, giving three total.

2. How many tarts have the side substitution?

Answer and explanation

B. One of the three

The specified substitution applies to one tart, not the entire ticket.

3. What does salad replace?

Answer and explanation

D. Potatoes

Salad is supplied instead of potatoes for the one modified tart.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Revised 41 refers to the same ticket, but the meaning of the new quantity still needs confirmation. Here, Cole explicitly says replaces. Three total is not two plus three, and one salad substitution does not apply to all three tarts.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
ticket number
Identifier linking an order and its updates.confirm the ticket number
revised ticket
Updated order record changing a previous instruction.read the revised ticket
replacement instruction
Direction that supersedes an earlier direction rather than adding to it.confirm a replacement instruction
additional order
New quantity added beyond the existing confirmed order.distinguish an additional order
current total
Complete quantity now required after applying the confirmed change.state the current total
superseded ticket
Earlier record no longer governing the current instruction.identify the superseded ticket
amendment
Change to an existing order or document.acknowledge the amendment
modifier
Instruction changing a particular item's standard preparation or accompaniment.attach the modifier
substitution
Replacement of one component with another.confirm the substitution
instead of
Phrase indicating replacement rather than addition.serve salad instead of potatoes
extra
Additional quantity beyond what is already included.distinguish extra from replacement
all day
Kitchen shorthand for the total quantity outstanding across the stated order scope.clarify the all-day count
expediter
Person coordinating order information and dish completion at the pass.confirm with the expediter
ticket rail
Place where order slips are held and organized for service.organize the ticket rail
duplicate production
Making an order twice because its records or instructions were misread.prevent duplicate production
active instruction
Direction currently governing the work.identify the active instruction
change acknowledgment
Explicit confirmation that a revision has been received and understood.give change acknowledgment
item identification
Details distinguishing the particular dish affected by a request.confirm item identification
seat mapping
Link between an ordered item and the guest's place at a table.verify seat mapping
side order
Accompanying item ordered alongside a main or other dish.clarify the side order
standard accompaniment
Side normally supplied with an item unless modified.identify the standard accompaniment
one-item change
Modification affecting one item rather than every item in the order.preserve the one-item change
order scope
Set of tickets or items to which a quantity or instruction applies.state the order scope
revision trail
Record showing how an instruction changed over time.preserve the revision trail

Salad instead of potatoes changes the side, not the tart count. Do not invent a seat number or report dishes ready from a quantity correction. Keep the current instruction identifiable and preserve any required revision history through the actual system.

C Notice the language

Repeat the whole corrected meaning

A quick yes can confirm the wrong interpretation if two people are looking at different slips. Repeat replaces, the ticket number, the current total, and the affected item. Keep the superseded record traceable through the actual system without treating it as another active order.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify both records
I have the original two-tart ticket and the revised slip for three.
Ask the key distinction
Does the revised quantity replace the old one or add to it?
Confirm replacement
It replaces the old quantity.
Give the total
Ticket 41 is three tarts total.
Reject the wrong addition
Not two plus three.
Specify the modification
One of the three has salad instead of potatoes.
Keep the other sides unchanged
The other two keep potatoes.
Avoid changing every plate
The salad substitution applies to one item only.
Check the affected item
Which guest gets the salad substitution?
Keep the number stable
Both slips refer to ticket 41.
Prevent two active orders
Do not treat the earlier slip as another order to produce.
Preserve the record
Keep the revision trail according to our system.
Acknowledge the change
Heard: three total, replacing the earlier two.
Keep production status separate
This correction does not mean the tarts are already ready.
Clarify the scope
I am confirming this ticket, not every tart outstanding across the kitchen.
Close with a read-back
Ticket 41: three total, one salad instead of potatoes, earlier quantity replaced.
6 language and precision notes
Replace or add
Names the two interpretations that could cause duplicate production.
Three total
States the final quantity explicitly.
One of the three
Limits the modification to a single item.
Instead of
Means substitution, not both sides automatically.
This ticket
Limits the count to its stated order scope.
Received and understood
Confirms the meaning of the change, not just receipt of paper.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. Revised ticket 41 replaces two with three total; one has salad instead of potatoes.

The read-back captures replacement, final quantity, and the one-item side substitution.

2. Which interpretation of instead of is correct?

Answer and explanation

B. Salad replaces potatoes for the specified tart.

Instead of marks replacement of the named component within the specified item's scope.

3. What is not established by the revised ticket?

Answer and explanation

C. That the three tarts are prepared and ready

A quantity instruction does not establish completed preparation or readiness.

4. What should the team avoid doing with the earlier slip?

Answer and explanation

D. Treating it as a second active order

Treating both versions as active would create duplicate production beyond the confirmed total.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Three total, not five

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Cook on the tart station / Cole: Expediter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • current total
  • one-item change
  • substitution
  • revision trail
  • item identification
  • change acknowledgment
  • duplicate production
  • superseded ticket
  • revised ticket
  • order scope
  1. Imani
    Cole, I have two slips for ticket forty-one. The original says two tarts, and the new one says three tarts, revised forty-one. Can I check the meaning before proceeding?
  2. Cole
    Yes. The 1 replaces the earlier quantity. We need three tarts total for ticket forty-one, not two from one slip and three from another.
  3. Imani
    Three altogether, then, not three more. I will not make the five I would get by adding both slips.
  4. Cole
    The 2 is three. Please read it as a replacement instruction so the earlier two do not remain a separate quantity to make.
  5. Imani
    I also see one tart has salad instead of potatoes. That changes one accompaniment, not the number of tarts, correct?
  6. Cole
    Correct. It is a 3. One tart has salad; the other two keep the standard potato accompaniment.
  7. Imani
    Which item gets the salad? I need the correct reference on the ticket before it goes to the pass.
  8. Cole
    Good. The 4 still needs to be clear through our ticket process. A correct quantity does not help if the side reaches the wrong diner.
  9. Imani
    The phrase instead of matters here. It does not mean I should put both salad and potatoes on the modified plate.
  10. Cole
    Exactly. The 5 replaces potatoes on that item. Do not expand it into an extra side for every guest or cancel the tart itself.
  11. Imani
    How do we mark the old slip in this system? I do not want another cook picking it up as a fresh order.
  12. Cole
    Handle it as the 6 through our actual system. Preserve the required history while making the current instruction unmistakable.
  13. Imani
    I will not add the two and three together. We should also keep the ticket number visible so this change is not applied to a different table.
  14. Cole
    Yes. The 7 is ticket forty-one. We are not giving an all-day count for every tart outstanding across the kitchen.
  15. Imani
    If someone asks for the all-day count, I will check the other live tickets too. These three belong to forty-one only.
  16. Cole
    Right. State the scope whenever it could be unclear. Our aim here is to prevent 8 from the two versions, not to change any other confirmed order.
  17. Imani
    My repeat is forty-one, three tarts total, one with salad instead of potatoes, and the earlier quantity replaced. I will confirm the affected item's identification through the system.
  18. Cole
    That is the 9 I need. You have the new total and the limited substitution, without adding an invented seat number.
  19. Imani
    I will update the working instruction accordingly. I am not calling the ticket ready; this conversation has clarified what we need to prepare.
  20. Cole
    Correct. Keep the 10 clear, and report production progress separately. Three required does not mean three cooked, plated, or released from the pass.

Answers and explanations
  1. revised ticket Turn 2. The revised ticket updates the existing order; Cole confirms that its three-tart quantity replaces the earlier two.
  2. current total Turn 4. The current total is three after replacement; adding the two slips would incorrectly produce five tarts.
  3. one-item change Turn 6. The one-item change applies the salad substitution to one of the three tarts, not to every item.
  4. item identification Turn 8. Item identification must connect the substitution to the correct tart through the actual process, without inventing a seat reference.
  5. substitution Turn 10. The substitution replaces potatoes with salad for the identified tart; it does not add both sides or change all plates.
  6. superseded ticket Turn 12. The superseded ticket is the earlier version no longer governing production; retaining history must not keep it active.
  7. order scope Turn 14. The order scope is ticket forty-one; its three-tart total is not a count of every outstanding tart in the kitchen.
  8. duplicate production Turn 16. Duplicate production would result from treating the original and revised quantities as separate active orders instead of one replacement.
  9. change acknowledgment Turn 18. Change acknowledgment confirms the replacement quantity and modification have been understood, not merely that a new slip arrived.
  10. revision trail Turn 20. The revision trail records how the instruction changed while leaving the current production status distinct from completed preparation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Imani: Cole, I have two slips for ticket forty-one. The original says two tarts, and the new one says three tarts, revised forty-one. Can I check the meaning before proceeding?
  2. Cole: Yes. The revised ticket replaces the earlier quantity. We need three tarts total for ticket forty-one, not two from one slip and three from another.
  3. Imani: Three altogether, then, not three more. I will not make the five I would get by adding both slips.
  4. Cole: The current total is three. Please read it as a replacement instruction so the earlier two do not remain a separate quantity to make.
  5. Imani: I also see one tart has salad instead of potatoes. That changes one accompaniment, not the number of tarts, correct?
  6. Cole: Correct. It is a one-item change. One tart has salad; the other two keep the standard potato accompaniment.
  7. Imani: Which item gets the salad? I need the correct reference on the ticket before it goes to the pass.
  8. Cole: Good. The item identification still needs to be clear through our ticket process. A correct quantity does not help if the side reaches the wrong diner.
  9. Imani: The phrase instead of matters here. It does not mean I should put both salad and potatoes on the modified plate.
  10. Cole: Exactly. The substitution replaces potatoes on that item. Do not expand it into an extra side for every guest or cancel the tart itself.
  11. Imani: How do we mark the old slip in this system? I do not want another cook picking it up as a fresh order.
  12. Cole: Handle it as the superseded ticket through our actual system. Preserve the required history while making the current instruction unmistakable.
  13. Imani: I will not add the two and three together. We should also keep the ticket number visible so this change is not applied to a different table.
  14. Cole: Yes. The order scope is ticket forty-one. We are not giving an all-day count for every tart outstanding across the kitchen.
  15. Imani: If someone asks for the all-day count, I will check the other live tickets too. These three belong to forty-one only.
  16. Cole: Right. State the scope whenever it could be unclear. Our aim here is to prevent duplicate production from the two versions, not to change any other confirmed order.
  17. Imani: My repeat is forty-one, three tarts total, one with salad instead of potatoes, and the earlier quantity replaced. I will confirm the affected item's identification through the system.
  18. Cole: That is the change acknowledgment I need. You have the new total and the limited substitution, without adding an invented seat number.
  19. Imani: I will update the working instruction accordingly. I am not calling the ticket ready; this conversation has clarified what we need to prepare.
  20. Cole: Correct. Keep the revision trail clear, and report production progress separately. Three required does not mean three cooked, plated, or released from the pass.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three total, not five

Revised ticket 41 from the case.

  1. Cook: Revised forty-one says three tarts. Does that replace the original two or add another three?
  2. Expediter: It replaces the original quantity. The table needs three tarts total on that ticket.
  3. Cook: Thanks. I also see salad instead of potatoes, but the note doesn't identify a quantity.
  4. Expediter: That substitution belongs to one tart only. The other two keep their standard potatoes.
  5. Cook: So three tarts total: one with salad, two with potatoes. Is that the complete revision?
  6. Expediter: Yes. Don't combine the old ticket quantity with the new one when you count orders.
  7. Cook: I'll use the revised ticket and tell the other station the original quantity was replaced.
  8. Expediter: Please identify forty-one in that message; another table has a separate order for two tarts.
  9. Cook: Understood. The revision changes forty-one only and leaves the other table's order untouched.
  10. Expediter: Correct. Read back the table and ticket if another correction comes through during service.
What does all day include?

A cook checks the scope of an order total.

  1. Cook: You called six soups all day. Does that include the two on the new takeaway ticket?
  2. Expediter: Yes, six includes those two. Four belong to dining-room tickets and two to takeaway.
  3. Cook: Thanks. I was about to add the takeaway pair again and report eight instead.
  4. Expediter: Keep the total at six. The separate packing instruction doesn't create two extra portions.
  5. Cook: Which ticket has the takeaway pair? I want the service format attached to the right order.
  6. Expediter: Ticket fifty-three. The other four portions are listed across the two dining-room tickets beside it.
  7. Cook: I'll repeat six total, with two assigned to takeaway ticket fifty-three and four for tables.
  8. Expediter: Exactly. Tell me if a cancellation arrives before treating that total as unchanged.
  9. Cook: No cancellation has reached me. I'll keep the current six unless you issue a revision.
  10. Expediter: Good. That gives both the quantity and the order split without counting anything twice.
Urgent is not specific

A cook clarifies an urgent verbal request.

  1. Expediter: I need a replacement tart on the fly for the table near the window.
  2. Cook: Which table number and ticket? There are three window tables in tonight's seating arrangement.
  3. Expediter: Table eighteen, ticket sixty. The original plate was the wrong dish and needs replacing.
  4. Cook: Thank you. Does the replacement keep the standard side, or is there a modification?
  5. Expediter: It needs salad instead of potatoes. That was on the original ticket for this diner.
  6. Cook: I'll read that back: replacement tart for eighteen, ticket sixty, salad instead of potatoes.
  7. Expediter: Correct. Please give me an estimate rather than assuming the urgency makes it immediately available.
  8. Cook: I'll check the station's current work and give you a realistic estimate for this replacement.
  9. Expediter: Thanks. I'll tell the server the request is accepted, with the estimate still pending.
  10. Cook: I'll call the ticket number with that estimate so it doesn't get attached elsewhere.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three total, not five
  1. Check the answers, then read the revised-ticket conversation. Stress three total, not three extra.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat ticket 41's one-item salad substitution, keeping the other two accompaniments unchanged.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Replace a different quantity

Ticket 52 originally lists four pies. A revised slip lists six, and the expediter confirms replacement, not addition. One pie has peas instead of carrots. No seat number or readiness is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cook: "The current total is ___ pies."

Answer and explanation

A. six

The confirmed replacement sets the total at six, not four plus six.

2. Expediter: "The old quantity is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. replaced

The revised slip supersedes the earlier quantity rather than adding to it.

3. Cook: "One pie has ___ instead of carrots."

Answer and explanation

A. peas

Peas is the stated substitute for the one modified pie.

4. Expediter: "Readiness is not ___."

Answer and explanation

C. established

The quantity correction does not establish that any pie is ready.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Reading tickets and modifications
Original fictional case: Ticket 41 originally requests two tarts. A new slip says "three tarts, revised 41." Cook Imani asks expediter Cole whether the new slip replaces or adds to the old quantity. Cole confirms that it replaces it: the total is three tarts, not five. One of the three has salad instead of potatoes. The conversation does not establish a seat number, cooking completion, or allergy. Imani must retain the one-item substitution, clarify the actual item identification through the ticket process, and prevent both versions from being treated as separate orders.
Language workshop: Three total, not five
Communication goal: Distinguish a replacement ticket from an added order and keep a one-item substitution linked to the current quantity.
Language guidance: Revised 41 refers to the same ticket, but the meaning of the new quantity still needs confirmation. Here, Cole explicitly says replaces. Three total is not two plus three, and one salad substitution does not apply to all three tarts.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have the original two-tart ticket and the revised slip for three. / Does the revised quantity replace the old one or add to it? / It replaces the old quantity. / Ticket 41 is three tarts total. / Not two plus three. / One of the three has salad instead of potatoes. / The other two keep potatoes. / The salad substitution applies to one item only. / Which guest gets the salad substitution? / Both slips refer to ticket 41. / Do not treat the earlier slip as another order to produce. / Keep the revision trail according to our system. / Heard: three total, replacing the earlier two. / This correction does not mean the tarts are already ready. / I am confirming this ticket, not every tart outstanding across the kitchen. / Ticket 41: three total, one salad instead of potatoes, earlier quantity replaced.
Vocabulary: ticket number: Identifier linking an order and its updates. / revised ticket: Updated order record changing a previous instruction. / replacement instruction: Direction that supersedes an earlier direction rather than adding to it. / additional order: New quantity added beyond the existing confirmed order. / current total: Complete quantity now required after applying the confirmed change. / superseded ticket: Earlier record no longer governing the current instruction. / amendment: Change to an existing order or document. / modifier: Instruction changing a particular item's standard preparation or accompaniment. / substitution: Replacement of one component with another. / instead of: Phrase indicating replacement rather than addition. / extra: Additional quantity beyond what is already included. / all day: Kitchen shorthand for the total quantity outstanding across the stated order scope. / expediter: Person coordinating order information and dish completion at the pass. / ticket rail: Place where order slips are held and organized for service. / duplicate production: Making an order twice because its records or instructions were misread. / active instruction: Direction currently governing the work. / change acknowledgment: Explicit confirmation that a revision has been received and understood. / item identification: Details distinguishing the particular dish affected by a request. / seat mapping: Link between an ordered item and the guest's place at a table. / side order: Accompanying item ordered alongside a main or other dish. / standard accompaniment: Side normally supplied with an item unless modified. / one-item change: Modification affecting one item rather than every item in the order. / order scope: Set of tickets or items to which a quantity or instruction applies. / revision trail: Record showing how an instruction changed over time.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Coordinating the passTwo plated, two still cooking+

Your goal

Answer a whole-ticket readiness question with a precise split of completed work, remaining work, and estimated timing.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Two plated, two still cooking

Ticket 28 contains four mains. Two are plated, and two are still at cook Rosa's station. Rosa estimates four more minutes for the remaining pair. Expediter Jules asks whether the whole table is ready. Rosa must report partial progress and the current estimate without calling all four ready, guaranteeing pickup in exactly four minutes, or inventing a dispatch decision. Plating alone does not establish that all relevant food checks have been completed. Actual holding, quality, and release arrangements remain governed by the kitchen process, not by this language exercise.

Jules: Expediter coordinating the pass / Rosa: Cook completing ticket 28

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many mains are on ticket 28?

Answer and explanation

D. Four

The brief supplies four mains as the complete ticket quantity.

2. How many are plated?

Answer and explanation

C. Two

Two mains are plated, while the other two remain at the station.

3. What is Rosa's estimate for the remaining pair?

Answer and explanation

A. Four more minutes

Rosa estimates four additional minutes for the pair still at the station.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Two plated describes partial progress. Four more minutes describes an estimate for the remaining pair. Neither statement alone makes the full ticket ready to run. Keep quantity, location, stage, and timing together.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pass
Area where dishes are checked and handed from kitchen to service staff.coordinate the pass
ticket readiness
Status of the complete order against the required ready-to-serve conditions.confirm ticket readiness
plated
Arranged on a plate, without alone certifying every readiness check.report two plated
cook station
Assigned kitchen work area responsible for particular preparation tasks.check the cook station
remaining pair
Two items still outstanding within the stated group.finish the remaining pair
partial progress
Work completed for some but not all of the required scope.report partial progress
whole-table call
Status message referring to every relevant dish for a table.make a whole-table call
pickup
Collection of dishes from the kitchen for service under the actual arrangement.confirm pickup
dispatch
Release or movement of prepared dishes toward service.coordinate dispatch
runner
Colleague taking dishes from the pass to the dining area.brief the runner
plating sequence
Order in which dishes are assembled on plates.coordinate the plating sequence
finishing step
Final preparation action before the next required stage.complete the finishing step
garnish
Food element used to finish or accompany a dish.check the garnish
quality check
Review against the relevant dish and service requirements.complete the quality check
holding arrangement
Approved method and conditions for keeping food before the next stage.follow the holding arrangement
readiness estimate
Approximate expectation for reaching a specified ready stage.communicate the readiness estimate
remaining work
Tasks still needed before the defined completion point.identify the remaining work
coordination point
Event or location where different stations align their work.agree the coordination point
synchronized service
Planned delivery of related dishes together or in an agreed sequence.coordinate synchronized service
release decision
Authorization or instruction to send dishes from the relevant stage.confirm the release decision
status call
Brief spoken report of progress or readiness.give a status call
update trigger
Change or condition prompting a fresh status message.identify an update trigger
ready-to-run
Local service status indicating dishes can be collected under the actual process.confirm ready-to-run status
full ticket
Entire order rather than one completed component.check the full ticket

Do not infer food safety or quality from the word plated. Actual holding limits, checks, and release decisions must follow the real kitchen process. If the estimate changes, update the expediter rather than allow an earlier approximate time to become a false promise.

C Notice the language

Answer the scope of the question

An expediter may need a table-level answer while a cook is focused on one station. Start with not the whole ticket yet, then give the exact split. Clear partial progress is more useful than either a vague yes or a vague not ready.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Answer the whole-ticket question
Not the whole ticket yet.
Give the total
Ticket 28 has four mains.
State completed progress
Two are plated.
Locate the remainder
The other two are still at my station.
Give the estimate
I estimate four more minutes for the remaining pair.
Keep the scope clear
That estimate is for the two outstanding mains.
Avoid a premature call
I am not calling all four ready.
Separate plating and release
Plated does not mean the full release check is complete.
Coordinate holding properly
We need to follow our actual holding and quality arrangements.
Avoid an invented dispatch
I have not called that ticket for pickup yet.
Make a useful repeat
Four total, two plated, two pending, around four more minutes.
Update a change
I will call you if that estimate changes.
Keep the runner informed
The runner needs the actual ready-to-run status.
Do not blur the count
Two at the pass is not four ready for the table.
Confirm the next stage
Let us verify the full ticket before the release call.
Close accurately
The table is partially prepared, not yet confirmed ready as a whole.
6 language and precision notes
Not the whole ticket
Corrects the scope immediately.
Two are, two are
Makes the split easy to hear during a busy service.
Still at my station
Gives a concrete location and incomplete stage.
I estimate
Identifies the speaker's forecast rather than a guaranteed time.
For the remaining pair
Limits the timing claim to the unfinished items.
If that changes
Creates an expectation of updated information.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which status call answers Jules accurately?

Answer and explanation

B. Ticket 28: four total, two plated, two at the station, estimated four more minutes for the pair.

The call preserves the full quantity, partial completion, location, and estimated remaining time.

2. What does plated establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

A. A preparation stage, not completion of every food and release check

Plating describes assembly and does not independently certify all required checks or later stages.

3. Which time statement overclaims?

Answer and explanation

A. The entire table will definitely be delivered in exactly four minutes.

The estimate concerns the remaining pair and cannot guarantee the timing of every later service stage.

4. What should happen if the estimate changes?

Answer and explanation

C. Tell the expediter the revised status.

Updated communication prevents a provisional earlier estimate from misleading the rest of the service team.

E Conversations

Two plated, two still cooking

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jules: Expediter coordinating the pass / Rosa: Cook completing ticket 28

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • update trigger
  • release decision
  • full ticket
  • Synchronized service
  • Ready-to-run
  • remaining pair
  • partial progress
  • status call
  • readiness estimate
  • holding arrangement
  1. Jules
    Rosa, twenty-eight: can I call the runner? I have four mains on the ticket but can only see two plates at the pass.
  2. Rosa
    Not yet. It is 1: two plated, two still at my station. Keep the full ticket open.
  3. Jules
    What is the wait on the other two? I need a clear update for the server, not a guess from looking at the plates.
  4. Rosa
    The 2 needs about four more minutes. I will call if that changes.
  5. Jules
    Four more from now, understood. That is preparation time for those two, not a promise that the runner will be at the table then.
  6. Rosa
    Correct. That is my 3, not a finished call. I am still working on the pair.
  7. Jules
    I will tell the server: four ordered, two plated, two still coming. I will not say the whole table is ready.
  8. Rosa
    Yes, keep the 4 in view. My station update covers only part of it.
  9. Jules
    The plated dishes need attention while we coordinate the rest. We cannot just leave them here indefinitely because a time was mentioned.
  10. Rosa
    Follow the agreed 5 and checks. Tell me promptly if the timing creates a problem for those dishes.
  11. Jules
    I have not sent either plate. Are you calling for pickup now, or are we still coordinating?
  12. Rosa
    Still coordinating. No 6 has been confirmed. I am not telling the runner to take the pair.
  13. Jules
    Understood. I need the dishes brought together through our service plan, with the relevant checks, not a stream of unrelated plates.
  14. Rosa
    7 is what we are coordinating. I will keep you informed about the two at my station.
  15. Jules
    When they are plated, say that first. Then we can verify the whole ticket before anyone gives the ready-to-run call.
  16. Rosa
    I will make the 8 specific. Two more plated is a different message from four checked and ready.
  17. Jules
    And if four minutes slips, call it straight away. I would rather update the server than leave them repeating an old time.
  18. Rosa
    That is the 9: a change to timing or readiness. I will not wait for you to chase.
  19. Jules
    My read-back: twenty-eight, four mains total, two plated, two at your station, around four more minutes for the pair. No pickup call yet.
  20. Rosa
    Correct. 10 is not confirmed for all four. I will give you the next actual change while we follow the kitchen's checks.

Answers and explanations
  1. partial progress Turn 2. Two of the four mains are plated, so the update reports partial progress rather than whole-ticket readiness.
  2. remaining pair Turn 4. The remaining pair is the two mains still at Rosa's station, distinct from the two plated dishes.
  3. readiness estimate Turn 6. Rosa's estimate is four additional minutes for the unfinished pair, not an exact table-delivery promise.
  4. full ticket Turn 8. The full ticket is all four mains; seeing two plated dishes does not establish completion of that scope.
  5. holding arrangement Turn 10. Holding must follow the actual kitchen's approved conditions; an estimated delay does not authorize arbitrary holding.
  6. release decision Turn 12. A release decision authorizes dispatch; this conversation has not established that decision for any part of the order.
  7. Synchronized service Turn 14. Synchronized service coordinates related dishes under the kitchen's actual plan rather than equating one station's progress with dispatch.
  8. status call Turn 16. The status call identifies the actual stage; plated and ready to run are not automatically interchangeable.
  9. update trigger Turn 18. A changed estimate or readiness problem triggers a fresh message before colleagues continue relying on stale information.
  10. Ready-to-run Turn 20. Ready-to-run means the actual collection-ready stage; it has not yet been established for all four mains.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jules: Rosa, twenty-eight: can I call the runner? I have four mains on the ticket but can only see two plates at the pass.
  2. Rosa: Not yet. It is partial progress: two plated, two still at my station. Keep the full ticket open.
  3. Jules: What is the wait on the other two? I need a clear update for the server, not a guess from looking at the plates.
  4. Rosa: The remaining pair needs about four more minutes. I will call if that changes.
  5. Jules: Four more from now, understood. That is preparation time for those two, not a promise that the runner will be at the table then.
  6. Rosa: Correct. That is my readiness estimate, not a finished call. I am still working on the pair.
  7. Jules: I will tell the server: four ordered, two plated, two still coming. I will not say the whole table is ready.
  8. Rosa: Yes, keep the full ticket in view. My station update covers only part of it.
  9. Jules: The plated dishes need attention while we coordinate the rest. We cannot just leave them here indefinitely because a time was mentioned.
  10. Rosa: Follow the agreed holding arrangement and checks. Tell me promptly if the timing creates a problem for those dishes.
  11. Jules: I have not sent either plate. Are you calling for pickup now, or are we still coordinating?
  12. Rosa: Still coordinating. No release decision has been confirmed. I am not telling the runner to take the pair.
  13. Jules: Understood. I need the dishes brought together through our service plan, with the relevant checks, not a stream of unrelated plates.
  14. Rosa: Synchronized service is what we are coordinating. I will keep you informed about the two at my station.
  15. Jules: When they are plated, say that first. Then we can verify the whole ticket before anyone gives the ready-to-run call.
  16. Rosa: I will make the status call specific. Two more plated is a different message from four checked and ready.
  17. Jules: And if four minutes slips, call it straight away. I would rather update the server than leave them repeating an old time.
  18. Rosa: That is the update trigger: a change to timing or readiness. I will not wait for you to chase.
  19. Jules: My read-back: twenty-eight, four mains total, two plated, two at your station, around four more minutes for the pair. No pickup call yet.
  20. Rosa: Correct. Ready-to-run is not confirmed for all four. I will give you the next actual change while we follow the kitchen's checks.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Half a ticket is not a pickup

Ticket 28 from the case.

  1. Expediter: Can I call the pickup for twenty-eight? I see two plated mains at the pass.
  2. Cook: Not yet. There are four mains on that ticket, and two are still at my station.
  3. Expediter: How long do you estimate for the remaining pair, taking your current work into account?
  4. Cook: Approximately four more minutes. I'll tell you if that changes before the full ticket is ready.
  5. Expediter: I'll keep the pickup open, then. Are those two plated dishes definitely for twenty-eight?
  6. Cook: Yes, their ticket is beside them. They aren't part of the neighboring order for twenty-nine.
  7. Expediter: Good. The runner nearly combined them with that order because the plates looked similar.
  8. Cook: I'll keep the table reference clear and call you when the remaining pair reaches the pass.
  9. Expediter: Please do. I'll coordinate the full pickup after checking all four against the ticket.
  10. Cook: Understood. Two plated, two pending, with the current estimate still four more minutes.
A garnish mismatch at the pass

An expediter questions a visible difference between a dish and its ticket.

  1. Expediter: This ticket says no herb garnish, but the plate here has herbs on top.
  2. Cook: Let me check the ticket number before assuming that plate belongs to the modified order.
  3. Expediter: It's thirty-four, seat two. The dish beside it is the standard version for seat one.
  4. Cook: You're right. I've matched the standard plate to the wrong seat at the pass.
  5. Expediter: What is the next step for the modified dish? I haven't released either plate.
  6. Cook: I'll check the preparation status with the station lead and give you a confirmed correction.
  7. Expediter: Please keep the modification attached to seat two rather than changing both dishes on this ticket.
  8. Cook: Understood. One standard dish for seat one, one no-garnish order for seat two.
  9. Expediter: Tell me when the corrected pickup is ready for checking; don't just call the whole table done.
  10. Cook: I will name thirty-four and the two seat assignments when I return with the update.
The runner needs the whole location

A cook repairs a vague pickup announcement.

  1. Runner: You called the back table, but there are several tables beyond the partition tonight.
  2. Cook: I meant table twenty-six. Let me give the full number rather than a location nickname.
  3. Runner: Thank you. Are all three dishes here for twenty-six, or does one belong elsewhere?
  4. Cook: Only the two marked plates are for twenty-six. The third belongs to table sixteen.
  5. Runner: Then I'll collect the two confirmed dishes and leave sixteen's plate beside its own ticket.
  6. Cook: Please check with the expediter first; they're coordinating whether twenty-six's side dish joins this pickup.
  7. Runner: I hadn't seen a side dish on the runner copy. I'll ask about that before leaving.
  8. Cook: Good. My call identified the two mains, not whether the entire order was complete.
  9. Runner: The expediter confirms the side is still pending and wants this pickup held for now.
  10. Cook: I'll keep the plates identified and let them make the next pickup call.

Answer and explanation

C. six

Six is the complete number required for ticket sixty-three.

2. Expediter: "The plated count is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. four

Four mains have reached plating, leaving two at the station.

3. Cook: "The remaining pair needs an estimated ___ more minutes."

Answer and explanation

A. three

Three additional minutes is the supplied estimate for the unfinished pair.

4. Expediter: "Whole-ticket release is not ___."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmed

Partial plating and an estimate do not establish the full release decision.

Lesson 04: Coordinating the pass
Original fictional case: Ticket 28 contains four mains. Two are plated, and two are still at cook Rosa's station. Rosa estimates four more minutes for the remaining pair. Expediter Jules asks whether the whole table is ready. Rosa must report partial progress and the current estimate without calling all four ready, guaranteeing pickup in exactly four minutes, or inventing a dispatch decision. Plating alone does not establish that all relevant food checks have been completed. Actual holding, quality, and release arrangements remain governed by the kitchen process, not by this language exercise.
Language workshop: Two plated, two still cooking
Communication goal: Answer a whole-ticket readiness question with a precise split of completed work, remaining work, and estimated timing.
Language guidance: Two plated describes partial progress. Four more minutes describes an estimate for the remaining pair. Neither statement alone makes the full ticket ready to run. Keep quantity, location, stage, and timing together.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Not the whole ticket yet. / Ticket 28 has four mains. / Two are plated. / The other two are still at my station. / I estimate four more minutes for the remaining pair. / That estimate is for the two outstanding mains. / I am not calling all four ready. / Plated does not mean the full release check is complete. / We need to follow our actual holding and quality arrangements. / I have not called that ticket for pickup yet. / Four total, two plated, two pending, around four more minutes. / I will call you if that estimate changes. / The runner needs the actual ready-to-run status. / Two at the pass is not four ready for the table. / Let us verify the full ticket before the release call. / The table is partially prepared, not yet confirmed ready as a whole.
Vocabulary: pass: Area where dishes are checked and handed from kitchen to service staff. / ticket readiness: Status of the complete order against the required ready-to-serve conditions. / plated: Arranged on a plate, without alone certifying every readiness check. / cook station: Assigned kitchen work area responsible for particular preparation tasks. / remaining pair: Two items still outstanding within the stated group. / partial progress: Work completed for some but not all of the required scope. / whole-table call: Status message referring to every relevant dish for a table. / pickup: Collection of dishes from the kitchen for service under the actual arrangement. / dispatch: Release or movement of prepared dishes toward service. / runner: Colleague taking dishes from the pass to the dining area. / plating sequence: Order in which dishes are assembled on plates. / finishing step: Final preparation action before the next required stage. / garnish: Food element used to finish or accompany a dish. / quality check: Review against the relevant dish and service requirements. / holding arrangement: Approved method and conditions for keeping food before the next stage. / readiness estimate: Approximate expectation for reaching a specified ready stage. / remaining work: Tasks still needed before the defined completion point. / coordination point: Event or location where different stations align their work. / synchronized service: Planned delivery of related dishes together or in an agreed sequence. / release decision: Authorization or instruction to send dishes from the relevant stage. / status call: Brief spoken report of progress or readiness. / update trigger: Change or condition prompting a fresh status message. / ready-to-run: Local service status indicating dishes can be collected under the actual process. / full ticket: Entire order rather than one completed component.

Give the last read-back without converting a four-minute estimate into a pickup promise. Complete and check the six-main transfer. Say four plated and two remaining, with three more minutes estimated for the pair.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Lesson 04: Coordinating the pass
Original fictional case: Ticket 28 contains four mains. Two are plated, and two are still at cook Rosa's station. Rosa estimates four more minutes for the remaining pair. Expediter Jules asks whether the whole table is ready. Rosa must report partial progress and the current estimate without calling all four ready, guaranteeing pickup in exactly four minutes, or inventing a dispatch decision. Plating alone does not establish that all relevant food checks have been completed. Actual holding, quality, and release arrangements remain governed by the kitchen process, not by this language exercise.
Language workshop: Two plated, two still cooking
Communication goal: Answer a whole-ticket readiness question with a precise split of completed work, remaining work, and estimated timing.
Language guidance: Two plated describes partial progress. Four more minutes describes an estimate for the remaining pair. Neither statement alone makes the full ticket ready to run. Keep quantity, location, stage, and timing together.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Not the whole ticket yet. / Ticket 28 has four mains. / Two are plated. / The other two are still at my station. / I estimate four more minutes for the remaining pair. / That estimate is for the two outstanding mains. / I am not calling all four ready. / Plated does not mean the full release check is complete. / We need to follow our actual holding and quality arrangements. / I have not called that ticket for pickup yet. / Four total, two plated, two pending, around four more minutes. / I will call you if that estimate changes. / The runner needs the actual ready-to-run status. / Two at the pass is not four ready for the table. / Let us verify the full ticket before the release call. / The table is partially prepared, not yet confirmed ready as a whole.
Vocabulary: pass: Area where dishes are checked and handed from kitchen to service staff. / ticket readiness: Status of the complete order against the required ready-to-serve conditions. / plated: Arranged on a plate, without alone certifying every readiness check. / cook station: Assigned kitchen work area responsible for particular preparation tasks. / remaining pair: Two items still outstanding within the stated group. / partial progress: Work completed for some but not all of the required scope. / whole-table call: Status message referring to every relevant dish for a table. / pickup: Collection of dishes from the kitchen for service under the actual arrangement. / dispatch: Release or movement of prepared dishes toward service. / runner: Colleague taking dishes from the pass to the dining area. / plating sequence: Order in which dishes are assembled on plates. / finishing step: Final preparation action before the next required stage. / garnish: Food element used to finish or accompany a dish. / quality check: Review against the relevant dish and service requirements. / holding arrangement: Approved method and conditions for keeping food before the next stage. / readiness estimate: Approximate expectation for reaching a specified ready stage. / remaining work: Tasks still needed before the defined completion point. / coordination point: Event or location where different stations align their work. / synchronized service: Planned delivery of related dishes together or in an agreed sequence. / release decision: Authorization or instruction to send dishes from the relevant stage. / status call: Brief spoken report of progress or readiness. / update trigger: Change or condition prompting a fresh status message. / ready-to-run: Local service status indicating dishes can be collected under the actual process. / full ticket: Entire order rather than one completed component.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the pass exchange, using a short pause between the total, plated count, and remaining pair. Switch roles. Give the last read-back without converting a four-minute estimate into a pickup promise. Complete and check the six-main transfer. Say four plated and two remaining, with three more minutes estimated for the pair.
Partner: Jules: Expediter coordinating the pass / Rosa: Cook completing ticket 28
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ticket 63 has six mains. Four are plated, and two remain at the station. The cook estimates three more minutes for that pair. No whole-ticket release is confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jules: And if four minutes slips, call it straight away. I would rather update the server than leave them repeating an old time.
Rosa: That is the update trigger: a change to timing or readiness. I will not wait for you to chase.
Jules: My read-back: twenty-eight, four mains total, two plated, two at your station, around four more minutes for the pair. No pickup call yet.
Rosa: Correct. Ready-to-run is not confirmed for all four. I will give you the next actual change while we follow the kitchen's checks.
END REFERENCE
05Communicating a shortage and an alternativeFive sides, three portions left+

Your goal

Quantify a shortfall, present a proposed alternative, and assign responsibility for obtaining the diners' decision.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Five sides, three portions left

Three portions of potatoes remain unallocated when a new ticket requests five potato sides. Rice is available as a proposed alternative for two diners, but they have not agreed to it. Cook Dario explains the shortage to server Mei, who can ask the diners and return with their decision. Dario cannot silently substitute rice or describe the whole ticket as ready. The case supplies no additional potato stock, replenishment time, price difference, or dietary suitability assessment for rice. The team must keep the two-portion gap and pending choice visible.

Dario: Cook managing the side station / Mei: Server handling the affected ticket

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many potato portions remain unallocated?

Answer and explanation

B. Three

The brief gives three potato portions available for allocation to the new ticket.

2. What is the shortfall against five requested portions?

Answer and explanation

C. Two portions

Five requested minus three available leaves two portions short.

3. Who can obtain the diners' decision about rice?

Answer and explanation

B. Mei, the server

Mei is the named server who can ask the diners and return with their choice.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The shortfall is two portions, not a complete absence of potatoes. Rice being available does not prove that two diners want it. Do not reduce portion sizes, borrow committed stock, or change prices without the actual authorized process.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
available stock
Quantity presently available under the actual stock and use conditions.confirm available stock
unallocated portion
Prepared or available portion not yet assigned to a specific order.count unallocated portions
allocation
Assignment of stock to a particular order or purpose.confirm the allocation
committed stock
Quantity already assigned to existing requirements.protect committed stock
shortfall
Amount by which supply is less than the stated requirement.quantify the shortfall
as-purchased weight
Ingredient weight in its purchased form before the specified trimming or preparation, often abbreviated AP weight.record the as-purchased weight
portion count
Number of defined servings available or required.update the portion count
par level
Target stock quantity used for planning under the establishment's system.review the par level
replenishment
Addition of stock to restore availability.confirm replenishment
stockout
Situation in which an item is unavailable for the relevant demand.report a stockout
eighty-six
Restaurant shorthand indicating an item is unavailable or should no longer be sold in the stated scope.communicate an eighty-six call
availability board
Shared record of current menu or ingredient availability.update the availability board
alternative side
Different accompaniment offered in place of the requested side.propose an alternative side
starch
Menu category including foods such as potatoes, rice, or pasta, without implying equivalence.compare the starch options
proposed substitution
Suggested replacement that has not yet been agreed.label the proposed substitution
accepted substitution
Replacement explicitly agreed through the appropriate process.record the accepted substitution
guest decision
Diner's choice after the relevant option and conditions are explained.obtain the guest decision
order hold
Pause while an unresolved order detail is clarified.maintain the order hold
yield percentage
Usable output divided by input quantity, multiplied by 100, for a defined preparation stage.calculate the yield percentage
reallocation
Change in which order receives a quantity of stock.authorize reallocation
yield variance
Difference between expected and actual usable output.investigate a yield variance
edible portion
Usable quantity after the specified preparation or trimming stage, often abbreviated EP.calculate the edible portion
portion control
Consistent serving amounts under the applicable recipe and service specification.maintain portion control
return message
Information brought back after a query or request is answered.request a return message

One starch is not automatically equivalent to another for preference, ingredients, or dietary requirements. The server should communicate the option accurately and verify any relevant unanswered question. No replenishment time or price consequence is established by this case.

C Notice the language

An alternative is an offer until accepted

Kitchen availability and guest preference are different parts of the decision. State the shortage early and give the server a clear option to discuss. A convenient alternative should not become a silent substitution, especially when price or dietary questions still require checking.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the available count
Three potato portions remain unallocated.
State the demand
The new ticket requests five.
Quantify the gap
We are two portions short.
Offer the actual alternative
Rice is available as a proposed alternative side.
Preserve the decision
The two diners have not accepted it yet.
Assign the question
Mei, can you ask the diners and bring back their choice?
Accept ownership
I will ask them and return with the decision.
Avoid a silent change
Do not substitute rice before the choice is confirmed.
Keep the remaining potatoes clear
The three available portions do not cover all five requests.
Avoid invented replenishment
We have no confirmed time for more potatoes.
Do not imply price equality
Check the price before offering it as a like-for-like change.
Keep dietary questions separate
Rice availability is not a dietary-suitability confirmation.
Retain the unresolved item
The two-side decision remains open.
Do not distort portions
A shortage does not authorize smaller portions without approval.
Ask for a precise return
Tell me which diners accept which side.
Close the handoff
Three potato portions available, two choices pending, Mei obtaining the decision.
6 language and precision notes
Unallocated
Distinguishes free stock from portions already promised elsewhere.
Short
States a numerical gap rather than blaming the preparation team.
Proposed
Keeps the alternative from sounding agreed.
Bring back
Makes the communication loop explicit.
Which diners
Prevents a correct replacement from reaching the wrong person.
No confirmed time
Avoids a replenishment promise unsupported by the case.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which shortage report is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Five requested, three unallocated potato portions, two short.

The comparison uses the stated demand and available count without exaggerating the shortage.

2. When may rice be described as accepted?

Answer and explanation

B. After the relevant diners' choice is actually confirmed

Availability establishes an option, not a guest decision or agreed substitution.

3. Which handoff closes the communication loop?

Answer and explanation

D. Mei will ask the diners and return with their choices.

Naming the person and required return message gives the pending decision a clear owner.

4. What is not supported by the case?

Answer and explanation

C. Rice has the same price and is suitable for every dietary need.

Neither a price comparison nor a dietary-suitability assessment is supplied merely by rice availability.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

Five sides, three portions left

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dario: Cook managing the side station / Mei: Server handling the affected ticket

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • order hold
  • return message
  • unallocated portion
  • proposed substitution
  • accepted substitution
  • alternative side
  • portion control
  • replenishment
  • guest decision
  • shortfall
  1. Dario
    Mei, I need to flag the side count before this new ticket moves on. It requests five potato portions, and we have only three left unallocated.
  2. Mei
    So the 1 is two portions. I should not tell the table that potatoes are completely gone, but we cannot cover all five requests.
  3. Dario
    Correct. Rice is available as an option for the other two sides, but nobody has asked the diners whether they want that change.
  4. Mei
    I will present it as a 2, not as something the kitchen has already decided for them. I need to return with their actual choices.
  5. Dario
    Those three portions are free for this ticket. I have already separated them from stock committed to other orders.
  6. Mei
    That makes the 3 count clear. We have three available for allocation, not five available and not three already committed to another table.
  7. Dario
    Exactly. I also have no confirmed time for more potatoes. Please do not tell the guests another batch will be ready shortly unless that is actually checked.
  8. Mei
    I will not promise 4 that has not been confirmed. My message will be the current count and the rice option, with the choice still open.
  9. Dario
    Mei, can you ask the table and bring the answer straight back? Please tell me exactly which two guests agree to rice.
  10. Mei
    Yes. I will obtain the 5 and return to you. I will identify the affected diners so a confirmed alternative is not attached to the wrong plate.
  11. Dario
    Good. Until then, I will not put rice on two plates and hope that saves time. The availability of an ingredient is not agreement to change the order.
  12. Mei
    Agreed. The 6 on that unresolved detail stays clear. We have an option to discuss, not a completed replacement instruction.
  13. Dario
    If they ask whether the price changes, I do not have that answer from the information we have. That needs the actual authorized pricing response.
  14. Mei
    I will separate that from the 7 itself. I also will not claim it meets a dietary requirement without the appropriate ingredient and preparation checks.
  15. Dario
    We also cannot make five by quietly shrinking the three portions. The serving specification has not changed.
  16. Mei
    The shortage does not change 8. I will explain the real option rather than hide the difference in the amount served.
  17. Dario
    If they accept rice, please bring back which two diners agreed. If they do not, tell me that too so we can use the proper next step.
  18. Mei
    I will make the 9 specific. Silence or the fact that I walked to the table will not count as acceptance.
  19. Dario
    Then we are aligned: five requested, three potato portions available, two pending choices, and you are asking about rice. The ticket is not complete yet.
  20. Mei
    Correct. An 10 will be recorded only after the relevant decision. I am taking the question now and will return with the answer.

Answers and explanations
  1. shortfall Turn 2. The shortfall is five requested minus three available, leaving two potato portions not covered by the current supply.
  2. proposed substitution Turn 4. The proposed substitution is an available rice option that remains unaccepted until the diners' relevant choice is confirmed.
  3. unallocated portion Turn 6. An unallocated portion has not been assigned to another order; three such portions are available in the stated count.
  4. replenishment Turn 8. Replenishment would add more stock, but the case supplies no confirmed additional quantity or timing.
  5. guest decision Turn 10. The guest decision belongs to the diners; Mei explicitly accepts the task of asking and reporting it back.
  6. order hold Turn 12. The order hold preserves the unresolved side choice; it prevents a proposed alternative from becoming a silent change.
  7. alternative side Turn 14. The alternative side is rice, but its availability does not establish a price consequence or settle dietary suitability.
  8. portion control Turn 16. Portion control preserves the specified serving amount; a shortage does not authorize silently shrinking portions to cover demand.
  9. return message Turn 18. The return message reports the actual choices and affected diners, completing the communication loop without assuming acceptance.
  10. accepted substitution Turn 20. An accepted substitution requires an actual confirmed choice; the present handoff establishes only the option and the person asking.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dario: Mei, I need to flag the side count before this new ticket moves on. It requests five potato portions, and we have only three left unallocated.
  2. Mei: So the shortfall is two portions. I should not tell the table that potatoes are completely gone, but we cannot cover all five requests.
  3. Dario: Correct. Rice is available as an option for the other two sides, but nobody has asked the diners whether they want that change.
  4. Mei: I will present it as a proposed substitution, not as something the kitchen has already decided for them. I need to return with their actual choices.
  5. Dario: Those three portions are free for this ticket. I have already separated them from stock committed to other orders.
  6. Mei: That makes the unallocated portion count clear. We have three available for allocation, not five available and not three already committed to another table.
  7. Dario: Exactly. I also have no confirmed time for more potatoes. Please do not tell the guests another batch will be ready shortly unless that is actually checked.
  8. Mei: I will not promise replenishment that has not been confirmed. My message will be the current count and the rice option, with the choice still open.
  9. Dario: Mei, can you ask the table and bring the answer straight back? Please tell me exactly which two guests agree to rice.
  10. Mei: Yes. I will obtain the guest decision and return to you. I will identify the affected diners so a confirmed alternative is not attached to the wrong plate.
  11. Dario: Good. Until then, I will not put rice on two plates and hope that saves time. The availability of an ingredient is not agreement to change the order.
  12. Mei: Agreed. The order hold on that unresolved detail stays clear. We have an option to discuss, not a completed replacement instruction.
  13. Dario: If they ask whether the price changes, I do not have that answer from the information we have. That needs the actual authorized pricing response.
  14. Mei: I will separate that from the alternative side itself. I also will not claim it meets a dietary requirement without the appropriate ingredient and preparation checks.
  15. Dario: We also cannot make five by quietly shrinking the three portions. The serving specification has not changed.
  16. Mei: The shortage does not change portion control. I will explain the real option rather than hide the difference in the amount served.
  17. Dario: If they accept rice, please bring back which two diners agreed. If they do not, tell me that too so we can use the proper next step.
  18. Mei: I will make the return message specific. Silence or the fact that I walked to the table will not count as acceptance.
  19. Dario: Then we are aligned: five requested, three potato portions available, two pending choices, and you are asking about rice. The ticket is not complete yet.
  20. Mei: Correct. An accepted substitution will be recorded only after the relevant decision. I am taking the question now and will return with the answer.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three portions for five orders

The potato shortage in the case.

  1. Cook: The potato portion count is three, but this ticket asks for five sides.
  2. Server: Could you put rice on the other two plates and let me explain afterward?
  3. Cook: Please ask first. Rice is available, but we don't know which diners would accept it.
  4. Server: You're right. I'll offer rice to the table and get the actual seat choices.
  5. Cook: Thank you. Keep the three available potatoes separate from any portions already committed elsewhere.
  6. Server: Do you have enough rice for both proposed changes if two diners choose it?
  7. Cook: Yes, two rice sides are available for this proposal. Neither substitution is confirmed yet.
  8. Server: I'll return with the names of the two seats, or tell you if they decline.
  9. Cook: Then I'll read back the five sides against those seats before the ticket proceeds.
  10. Server: Agreed. I'll describe the shortage clearly rather than call rice the standard accompaniment.
A stock word needs a scope

A cook clarifies whether a shortage concerns one dish or an ingredient.

  1. Cook: You called the mushrooms eighty-six. Do you mean the mushroom starter or every dish containing mushrooms?
  2. Expediter: The mushroom starter only. The pasta station has its own allocated ingredient stock for service.
  3. Cook: Thanks. I'll tell the servers the starter is unavailable, not remove the pasta from their options.
  4. Expediter: Please use the full dish name. The shortened call caused confusion at the other station too.
  5. Cook: Is the starter unavailable for the rest of this service, or just while another batch is checked?
  6. Expediter: The lead has marked it unavailable for tonight. No further starter batch is planned.
  7. Cook: I'll repeat that as mushroom starter unavailable tonight, with mushroom pasta availability unchanged.
  8. Expediter: Exactly. The separate allocation is why the dish name matters more than the ingredient nickname.
  9. Cook: I'll also check earlier starter orders before discussing replacements.
  10. Expediter: The lead is checking earlier tickets separately; the starter is unavailable for new orders tonight.
A par level is not an order

A prep cook questions a replenishment request.

  1. Prep cook: The par level says forty portions, but we have twenty and only ten orders expected.
  2. Lead cook: Are you asking whether to prepare twenty more just to reach the reference level?
  3. Prep cook: Yes. I don't want to treat the printed par figure as a new production instruction.
  4. Lead cook: That's sensible. Today's approved prep target stays at twenty because the service plan is smaller.
  5. Prep cook: Then the existing twenty meet today's target, even though they're below the usual par level.
  6. Lead cook: Correct. The par is a planning reference, not an automatic instruction for every shift.
  7. Prep cook: Should I tell the incoming cook why the board shows a difference from that reference?
  8. Lead cook: Please do. Explain the reduced target so they don't add an unnecessary batch later.
  9. Prep cook: I'll say twenty available against today's target of twenty, with the usual par unchanged at forty.
  10. Lead cook: Exactly. Any further production needs a new decision based on the service update, not the gap alone.

No diner has confirmed acceptance in the supplied facts.

Mei: Correct. An accepted substitution will be recorded only after the relevant decision. I am taking the question now and will return with the answer.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
