Lesson 05: Communicating a shortage and an alternative
Original fictional case: Three portions of potatoes remain unallocated when a new ticket requests five potato sides. Rice is available as a proposed alternative for two diners, but they have not agreed to it. Cook Dario explains the shortage to server Mei, who can ask the diners and return with their decision. Dario cannot silently substitute rice or describe the whole ticket as ready. The case supplies no additional potato stock, replenishment time, price difference, or dietary suitability assessment for rice. The team must keep the two-portion gap and pending choice visible.
Language workshop: Five sides, three portions left
Communication goal: Quantify a shortfall, present a proposed alternative, and assign responsibility for obtaining the diners' decision.
Language guidance: The shortfall is two portions, not a complete absence of potatoes. Rice being available does not prove that two diners want it. Do not reduce portion sizes, borrow committed stock, or change prices without the actual authorized process.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Three potato portions remain unallocated. / The new ticket requests five. / We are two portions short. / Rice is available as a proposed alternative side. / The two diners have not accepted it yet. / Mei, can you ask the diners and bring back their choice? / I will ask them and return with the decision. / Do not substitute rice before the choice is confirmed. / The three available portions do not cover all five requests. / We have no confirmed time for more potatoes. / Check the price before offering it as a like-for-like change. / Rice availability is not a dietary-suitability confirmation. / The two-side decision remains open. / A shortage does not authorize smaller portions without approval. / Tell me which diners accept which side. / Three potato portions available, two choices pending, Mei obtaining the decision.
Vocabulary: available stock: Quantity presently available under the actual stock and use conditions. / unallocated portion: Prepared or available portion not yet assigned to a specific order. / allocation: Assignment of stock to a particular order or purpose. / committed stock: Quantity already assigned to existing requirements. / shortfall: Amount by which supply is less than the stated requirement. / as-purchased weight: Ingredient weight in its purchased form before the specified trimming or preparation, often abbreviated AP weight. / portion count: Number of defined servings available or required. / par level: Target stock quantity used for planning under the establishment's system. / replenishment: Addition of stock to restore availability. / stockout: Situation in which an item is unavailable for the relevant demand. / eighty-six: Restaurant shorthand indicating an item is unavailable or should no longer be sold in the stated scope. / availability board: Shared record of current menu or ingredient availability. / alternative side: Different accompaniment offered in place of the requested side. / starch: Menu category including foods such as potatoes, rice, or pasta, without implying equivalence. / proposed substitution: Suggested replacement that has not yet been agreed. / accepted substitution: Replacement explicitly agreed through the appropriate process. / guest decision: Diner's choice after the relevant option and conditions are explained. / order hold: Pause while an unresolved order detail is clarified. / yield percentage: Usable output divided by input quantity, multiplied by 100, for a defined preparation stage. / reallocation: Change in which order receives a quantity of stock. / yield variance: Difference between expected and actual usable output. / edible portion: Usable quantity after the specified preparation or trimming stage, often abbreviated EP. / portion control: Consistent serving amounts under the applicable recipe and service specification. / return message: Information brought back after a query or request is answered.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the shortage exchange and stress five requested, three available, two short. Switch roles. Keep rice as an offer until Mei reports the affected diners' actual decisions. Complete and check the couscous transfer. Read the carrot proposal without inventing acceptance, a price, or dietary suitability.
Partner: Dario: Cook managing the side station / Mei: Server handling the affected ticket
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A ticket requests seven portions of couscous, but five remain unallocated. Roasted carrots are available as a proposed alternative. Server Noor will ask the two affected diners. No acceptance or price decision is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dario: If they accept rice, please bring back which two diners agreed. If they do not, tell me that too so we can use the proper next step.
Mei: I will make the return message specific. Silence or the fact that I walked to the table will not count as acceptance.
Dario: Then we are aligned: five requested, three potato portions available, two pending choices, and you are asking about rice. The ticket is not complete yet.
Mei: Correct. An accepted substitution will be recorded only after the relevant decision. I am taking the question now and will return with the answer.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Checking dietary modificationsNo cheese is not the full question+

Your goal

Upgrade a simple omission request to an accurately communicated allergy review without inventing ingredient or handling assurances.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

No cheese is not the full question

Ticket 17 says "no cheese." Server Arun then reports that the diner has a milk allergy. Cook Tessa has not checked the sauce specification and cannot confirm the dish's suitability. Lead Noor is available to review the question with the server. Tessa must preserve both the requested cheese omission and the newly reported milk allergy, keep the sauce and preparation questions open, and route them through the actual allergy process. No ingredient finding, accepted alternative, food exposure, or reaction is supplied. The exchange is a pre-service clarification, not a clinical assessment.

Arun: Server carrying the allergy report / Tessa: Cook receiving the updated ticket information

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What additional information does Arun report?

Answer and explanation

C. The diner has a milk allergy.

Arun reports a milk allergy beyond the original no-cheese wording.

2. Which specification remains unchecked?

Answer and explanation

C. The sauce specification

Tessa has not checked the sauce specification, so its relevant contents remain unverified.

3. Who is available to review the question?

Answer and explanation

B. Lead Noor with the server

Noor is available to review the question with Arun through the actual process.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Leaving off cheese answers a topping instruction, not the whole milk-allergy question. The sauce, other components, and preparation conditions require appropriate verification. Casein and whey are examples of milk-derived proteins; do not rely only on the everyday word milk appearing in a dish name.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
omission request
Instruction to leave out a named component.record the omission request
reported allergy
Allergy information attributed to the diner or relaying colleague.communicate the reported allergy
milk protein
Protein originating from milk that matters in milk-allergy assessment.identify milk protein
casein
Group of proteins found in milk and used in some food ingredients.recognize casein as milk-derived
whey protein
Milk-derived protein associated with the liquid separated during cheesemaking.identify whey protein
compound ingredient
Ingredient made from multiple components that may need separate checking.examine a compound ingredient
sauce specification
Documented information describing the actual sauce product or recipe.verify the sauce specification
ingredient declaration
Statement identifying ingredients in a food product.review the ingredient declaration
allergen source
Food source associated with a relevant allergenic protein.identify the allergen source
contains statement
Label statement identifying major food allergens intentionally present under the relevant labeling rules.read the contains statement
advisory statement
Warning such as may contain that requires appropriate interpretation, not a guarantee from its absence.interpret an advisory statement
recipe verification
Check that the relevant current recipe and ingredients are correctly identified.complete recipe verification
cross-contact
Unintended transfer or introduction of an allergen into another food.address cross-contact
shared utensil
Tool used across foods that may create a relevant handling question.check shared-utensil use
preparation control
Required measure governing how food is prepared under the actual safety system.follow preparation controls
allergen flag
Clear marker carrying the reported allergen with the relevant order.preserve the allergen flag
ticket update
Change adding or correcting information on the order record.communicate the ticket update
dish suitability
Whether the complete dish and preparation can meet the relevant verified requirement.assess dish suitability
unverified component
Part of a dish whose relevant information has not been checked.identify an unverified component
ingredient finding
Result actually established by checking a relevant ingredient source.report an ingredient finding
safety assurance
Statement that a specified safety condition is met, requiring adequate evidence and authority.avoid an unsupported safety assurance
pre-service review
Check performed before the relevant food is served.request a pre-service review
review owner
Person responsible for coordinating the unresolved question.identify the review owner
guest update
Accurate message to the diner about the query and its actual status.provide a guest update

No sauce result is supplied. Do not change unchecked into contains milk or milk-free. An absent advisory statement is not a universal absence guarantee. Use the actual current product information and allergy process, and communicate what remains unresolved.

C Notice the language

New information can change the question

No cheese may begin as an omission instruction, but a later allergy report requires clear communication of the allergen and the relevant review. Do not criticize the timing of the disclosure or reduce the new information back to a preference. Keep the guest informed without promising suitability.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Receive the update
Thank you; I have heard that the diner reports a milk allergy.
Keep the omission
The no-cheese request still belongs to ticket 17.
Expand the question accurately
Leaving off cheese does not confirm the whole dish.
Name the unchecked component
The sauce specification has not been checked.
Avoid a guessed ingredient
I cannot say whether this sauce contains milk from the information available.
Name the review owner
Noor can review the question with you.
Preserve the allergy marker
Keep milk allergy clearly linked to the ticket.
Ask about the actual recipe
We need the current sauce recipe or product information.
Include preparation
The relevant cross-contact and handling questions also need review.
Avoid a false safety claim
I cannot confirm suitability yet.
Keep the guest informed
Tell the diner the question is being checked, not already resolved.
Do not invent an alternative
Any replacement needs its own appropriate checks and the guest's choice.
Separate receipt and result
Receiving the allergy report is not completing the review.
Avoid blaming the disclosure
Let us handle the updated information clearly now.
Preserve the source
Arun reports the diner's milk allergy.
Close the handoff
Ticket 17, no cheese, milk allergy reported, sauce unchecked, Noor available to review.
6 language and precision notes
Still belongs
Preserves the original omission alongside new information.
Does not confirm
Rejects an unjustified leap from one modification to whole-dish suitability.
Has not been checked
Marks an information gap, not a positive or negative finding.
Current
Keeps the review tied to the actual recipe or product.
Also
Includes preparation questions beyond ingredients alone.
Not already resolved
Prevents a progress update from becoming reassurance.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement correctly handles the new report?

Answer and explanation

B. Keep no cheese and add the reported milk allergy for the appropriate review.

The update preserves the omission and communicates the new allergen information accurately.

2. What does unchecked sauce mean?

Answer and explanation

C. No relevant ingredient conclusion is established yet.

An unchecked specification supports neither a positive ingredient finding nor an absence assurance.

3. Which statement about casein and whey is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. They are examples of milk-derived proteins relevant to ingredient checking.

These terms identify milk-derived proteins but do not by themselves complete a whole-dish or preparation assessment.

4. Which guest update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. The milk-allergy question is under review; suitability is not confirmed yet.

The message communicates the actual unresolved stage without a false guarantee or invented event.

Answers and explanations
  1. omission request Turn 2. The omission request is no cheese; it remains relevant but does not replace the broader reported milk-allergy information.
  2. sauce specification Turn 4. The sauce specification has not been checked, so its relevant ingredients cannot yet be stated as present or absent.
  3. allergen flag Turn 6. The allergen flag keeps the reported milk allergy attached to ticket seventeen as it moves between the server, cook, and lead.
  4. unverified component Turn 8. An unverified component has not been checked; that status does not establish either milk content or absence.
  5. Recipe verification Turn 10. Recipe verification checks the relevant current recipe and ingredients, rather than relying on recollection or a different sauce version.
  6. compound ingredient Turn 12. A compound ingredient contains multiple components, so its relevant subingredients may need checking rather than relying on its short name.
  7. Cross-contact Turn 14. Cross-contact concerns unintended allergen introduction during handling or preparation, which remains distinct from intentional ingredients.
  8. guest update Turn 16. The guest update should describe the actual review stage without converting receipt of the allergy report into a completed suitability decision.
  9. dish suitability Turn 18. Dish suitability concerns the complete relevant dish and preparation, so another menu item needs its own appropriate assessment.
  10. review owner Turn 20. The review owner coordinates the unresolved question; Noor's availability does not itself complete the checks or authorize service.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A modifier is not an allergy clearance

Ticket 17 from the case.

  1. Server: The no-cheese request on seventeen is about a milk allergy, not a taste preference.
  2. Cook: Thanks for the new information. Omitting cheese doesn't answer whether the sauce contains milk ingredients.
  3. Server: I had described the dish as suitable once the cheese was removed. That was too broad.
  4. Cook: Please correct that with the guest. The sauce specification and handling question still need the lead's review.
  5. Server: I'll keep the order pending and explain that we need to check the whole preparation.
  6. Cook: I'll give the lead the ticket number and the guest's exact reported concern about milk.
  7. Server: Should I bring the guest another menu while the lead gathers the relevant information?
  8. Cook: You can offer one, but don't name another dish as suitable without its own check.
  9. Server: Understood. I'll explain the unresolved question and invite the lead to discuss it directly with them.
  10. Cook: That lets the diner choose using actual information, not our assumption.
A recipe version matters

A cook finds two ingredient sheets for the same sauce.

  1. Cook: There are two recipe specifications for the sauce, and their ingredient lists don't match.
  2. Lead cook: Are the documents dated, and can you identify which one the current batch refers to?
  3. Cook: The sheets have different dates, but I can't confirm which was used for this batch.
  4. Lead cook: Then don't answer the server from whichever sheet looks newer. We need the batch's actual information.
  5. Cook: I'll keep the suitability question unresolved and show you the two sheets and batch reference.
  6. Lead cook: Please do. What exactly did the diner ask so we check the right concern?
  7. Cook: They asked whether the sauce contains mustard. The server has not reported an allergy or preference yet.
  8. Lead cook: I'll ask the server to clarify that too, without delaying the ingredient-record check you've raised.
  9. Cook: So the batch's allergen declaration and the guest's exact concern both need checking?
  10. Lead cook: I'll handle the recipe review; please obtain the guest's exact wording.
A vegetarian label needs confirmation

A cook questions a proposed menu description without offering dietary certification.

  1. Server: A guest wants the soup without the meat garnish. Can I describe that as vegetarian?
  2. Cook: Not from that change alone. The base recipe matters, and I haven't checked its stock ingredients.
  3. Server: The garnish is the only meat I can see, which is why I thought it worked.
  4. Cook: Visible ingredients don't describe the whole recipe. Let's get the current specification from the lead.
  5. Server: I'll tell the guest removing the garnish is a request, not confirmation of the soup's composition.
  6. Cook: Good. Did they ask about a dietary preference, or report a specific allergy we should relay?
  7. Server: They stated a vegetarian preference. They didn't mention an allergy, and I won't add that label.
  8. Cook: Please pass on that exact preference while we check whether this recipe meets the request.
  9. Server: I'll leave the soup unconfirmed and discuss other choices only with verified ingredient information.
  10. Cook: That avoids turning an appearance-based guess into a menu claim.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

No cheese is not the full question
  1. Check the answers. Read the allergy update, preserving ticket 17, no cheese, and the reported milk allergy.
  2. Switch roles. Clearly distinguish an unchecked sauce from a verified milk finding and a completed whole-dish review.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Expand a different omission request

Ticket 35 says no peanuts on top. The server then reports a peanut allergy. The dressing specification is unchecked. Lead Maya is available to review the question. No suitability result is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Server: "The reported allergen is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. peanut

The reported allergy concerns peanut and must be preserved accurately.

2. Cook: "The unchecked component is the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. dressing

The dressing specification is explicitly the unresolved ingredient source.

3. Server: "The available review lead is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Maya

Maya is named as available to review the updated question.

4. Cook: "Suitability is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. confirmed

Removing a topping does not supply a completed whole-dish assessment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Checking dietary modifications
Original fictional case: Ticket 17 says "no cheese." Server Arun then reports that the diner has a milk allergy. Cook Tessa has not checked the sauce specification and cannot confirm the dish's suitability. Lead Noor is available to review the question with the server. Tessa must preserve both the requested cheese omission and the newly reported milk allergy, keep the sauce and preparation questions open, and route them through the actual allergy process. No ingredient finding, accepted alternative, food exposure, or reaction is supplied. The exchange is a pre-service clarification, not a clinical assessment.
Language workshop: No cheese is not the full question
Communication goal: Upgrade a simple omission request to an accurately communicated allergy review without inventing ingredient or handling assurances.
Language guidance: Leaving off cheese answers a topping instruction, not the whole milk-allergy question. The sauce, other components, and preparation conditions require appropriate verification. Casein and whey are examples of milk-derived proteins; do not rely only on the everyday word milk appearing in a dish name.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you; I have heard that the diner reports a milk allergy. / The no-cheese request still belongs to ticket 17. / Leaving off cheese does not confirm the whole dish. / The sauce specification has not been checked. / I cannot say whether this sauce contains milk from the information available. / Noor can review the question with you. / Keep milk allergy clearly linked to the ticket. / We need the current sauce recipe or product information. / The relevant cross-contact and handling questions also need review. / I cannot confirm suitability yet. / Tell the diner the question is being checked, not already resolved. / Any replacement needs its own appropriate checks and the guest's choice. / Receiving the allergy report is not completing the review. / Let us handle the updated information clearly now. / Arun reports the diner's milk allergy. / Ticket 17, no cheese, milk allergy reported, sauce unchecked, Noor available to review.

from milk that matters in milk-allergy assessment. / casein: Group of proteins found in milk and used in some food ingredients. / whey protein: Milk-derived protein associated with the liquid separated during cheesemaking. / compound ingredient: Ingredient made from multiple components that may need separate checking. / sauce specification: Documented information describing the actual sauce product or recipe. / ingredient declaration: Statement identifying ingredients in a food product. / allergen source: Food source associated with a relevant allergenic protein. / contains statement: Label statement identifying major food allergens intentionally present under the relevant labeling rules. / advisory statement: Warning such as may contain that requires appropriate interpretation, not a guarantee from its absence. / recipe verification: Check that the relevant current recipe and ingredients are correctly identified. / cross-contact: Unintended transfer or introduction of an allergen into another food. / shared utensil: Tool used across foods that may create a relevant handling question. / preparation control: Required measure governing how food is prepared under the actual safety system. / allergen flag: Clear marker carrying the reported allergen with the relevant order. / ticket update: Change adding or correcting information on the order record. / dish suitability: Whether the complete dish and preparation can meet the relevant verified requirement. / unverified component: Part of a dish whose relevant information has not been checked. / ingredient finding: Result actually established by checking a relevant ingredient source. / safety assurance: Statement that a specified safety condition is met, requiring adequate evidence and authority. / pre-service review: Check performed before the relevant food is served. / review owner: Person responsible for coordinating the unresolved question. / guest update: Accurate message to the diner about the query and its actual status.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Reporting equipment and dish-station needsScale B keeps going blank+

Your goal

Report an observable equipment problem, identify the affected work, and request review and a verified alternative.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Scale B keeps going blank

At the pastry prep bench, scale B repeatedly shows a blank display. Cook Nia has stopped using it. No cause, repair, or measurement error has been established. Lead Omar needs to arrange the appropriate review and confirm whether scale A is available and suitable for the task. Nia should name the equipment and location, describe the observed display behavior, and keep both review and alternative status clear. The case supplies no permission to repair scale B, no proof that previous batches are defective, and no confirmed time for returning it to use.

Nia: Cook at the pastry prep bench / Omar: Kitchen lead receiving the equipment report

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which equipment is affected?

Answer and explanation

D. Scale B at the pastry prep bench

The report concerns scale B at the named pastry preparation location.

2. What has Nia observed?

Answer and explanation

C. The display repeatedly goes blank.

The supplied symptom is a repeatedly blank display, not a diagnosis or proven batch defect.

3. What is still needed about scale A?

Answer and explanation

A. Confirmation that it is available and suitable for the task

Omar must verify the alternative rather than treat its mere existence as availability or suitability.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The display keeps going blank is an observation. The power supply has failed is a cause claim not supplied by this case. Describe the symptom without opening, adjusting, or repairing equipment outside the actual authorized process.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
equipment identifier
Name or code distinguishing a particular piece of equipment.state the equipment identifier
asset tag
Label used to identify equipment within an organization's records.check the asset tag
fault report
Account of an equipment problem and its relevant context.submit a fault report
display
Screen or indicator presenting a device's readings or status.describe the blank display
intermittent fault
Problem that appears repeatedly but not necessarily continuously.report an intermittent fault
observed symptom
Behavior directly noticed before its cause is established.state the observed symptom
diagnosis
Established explanation of a fault after appropriate assessment.avoid an unsupported diagnosis
out-of-use status
State indicating equipment has been removed from use under the relevant process.communicate out-of-use status
pastry bench
Work surface or station used for pastry preparation.identify the pastry bench
weighing task
Work requiring measurement of ingredient mass.pause the weighing task
measurement result
Value produced by a measurement process.verify the measurement result
measurement uncertainty
Quantified spread associated with a measurement result, not a known measurement error.report measurement uncertainty
resolution
Smallest displayed or distinguishable increment of a measuring device.check the scale resolution
capacity
Maximum load or operating range specified for the equipment.verify scale capacity
calibration
Comparison against a reference to establish measurement relationships, not necessarily adjustment.review calibration information
verification
Check that specified requirements are met.complete equipment verification
adjustment
Intervention changing equipment response to meet a required condition.distinguish adjustment from calibration
service request
Request for authorized equipment assessment or maintenance.raise a service request
authorized technician
Person permitted and qualified under the relevant arrangement to assess or service equipment.contact an authorized technician
alternative equipment
Different device proposed for the required task.verify alternative equipment
equipment availability
Whether a device can actually be assigned for use when needed.confirm equipment availability
fit for task
Suitable for the specific intended work under applicable requirements.confirm fit-for-task status
return to use
Resumption of operation after the required assessment and authorization.authorize return to use
review status
Current stage of the assessment, distinct from a completed repair.report review status

Scale A exists does not mean it is available, appropriate, or checked for this weighing task. Availability and suitability both need confirmation. A display fault also does not establish that every earlier ingredient weight or finished batch was wrong.

C Notice the language

Describe the symptom before the cause

A useful fault report identifies the equipment, location, observed behavior, and work already stopped. Avoid presenting a guess as a diagnosis. Ask for the specific support needed so the lead can route the problem and assess an alternative through the actual procedure.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the device
Scale B at the pastry prep bench is affected.
Describe the symptom
Its display keeps going blank.
State the action already taken
I have stopped using it.
Avoid a guessed cause
I do not know the cause.
Name the affected work
The weighing task cannot continue on this scale as normal.
Request review
Please arrange the appropriate equipment review.
Ask about the alternative
Can you confirm whether scale A is available and suitable?
Do not claim a repair
No repair has been completed.
Keep the status clear
Scale B has not been cleared for return to use.
Separate previous-work questions
We have not established an error in earlier batches.
Preserve the location
The report concerns the pastry bench, not an unspecified station.
Stay within authority
I will not attempt an unauthorized repair.
Keep alternative status honest
Please check scale A before we move the weighing task.
Ask for the actual next owner
Who will receive the service request?
Avoid a guessed timeline
No return-to-use time has been confirmed.
Close the report
Scale B, pastry bench, repeated blank display, use stopped, review needed.
6 language and precision notes
Keeps going
Describes repetition without inventing an exact frequency.
I have stopped
States an action already taken, not merely proposed.
Do not know
Separates observation from diagnosis.
Whether
Leaves the alternative genuinely open pending confirmation.
Available and suitable
Names two different checks.
Has not been cleared
Preserves the current operating status until the actual process changes it.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is strongest?

Answer and explanation

B. Scale B at the pastry bench repeatedly goes blank; I stopped using it; the cause is unknown.

The report names equipment, location, symptom, action, and uncertainty without an unsupported diagnosis.

2. Which request about scale A is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

C. Please verify that it is available and suitable for this task.

The alternative needs both practical availability and task suitability, not merely a known name.

3. What does the fault establish about previous batches?

Answer and explanation

A. No specific previous-batch error by itself

A display symptom alone does not determine previous measurement results or eliminate appropriate follow-up questions.

4. Which statement falsely reports completion?

Answer and explanation

D. Scale B is repaired and cleared for use.

The case supplies no completed repair, verification, or authorization to return scale B to use.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Scale B keeps going blank

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Cook at the pastry prep bench / Omar: Kitchen lead receiving the equipment report

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Equipment availability
  • fault report
  • return to use
  • out-of-use status
  • measurement result
  • fit for task
  • review status
  • service request
  • observed symptom
  • equipment identifier
  1. Nia
    Omar, I need help with scale B at the pastry prep bench. Its display keeps going blank, so I have stopped using it for the weighing task.
  2. Omar
    Thank you for making the 1 specific. Scale B, pastry prep bench, repeated blank display, and use stopped. Do you know the cause, or only the symptom?
  3. Nia
    Only the display problem. It has happened repeatedly. I have stopped using B and have not opened it or tried a repair.
  4. Omar
    Keep that as an 2. We should not write that a particular component failed when we have not established it through the appropriate assessment.
  5. Nia
    Can you arrange the equipment review and check whether scale A is available? I need a confirmed way forward for the work at this bench.
  6. Omar
    I will arrange the appropriate 3. I also need to check scale A rather than assume it can be assigned to you immediately.
  7. Nia
    I saw scale A earlier, but another station may need it. Could you check that as well as whether it fits this weighing task?
  8. Omar
    Exactly. 4 is one question, and suitability is another. We need both answered before presenting it as your confirmed alternative.
  9. Nia
    Please keep the location in the report. A message saying the scale is blank could send someone to a different bench or device.
  10. Omar
    I will include the 5 and the pastry location. That gives the person reviewing it a clear starting point without a guessed cause.
  11. Nia
    Does this automatically mean that everything I weighed earlier was wrong? I do not have evidence about those earlier readings from this symptom alone.
  12. Omar
    It does not establish a specific 6 as wrong. Any question about earlier work needs its own appropriate review, not a blanket conclusion or a blanket reassurance.
  13. Nia
    I will not resume using scale B just because the display briefly comes back. The repeated blanking is the problem I am reporting.
  14. Omar
    Keep the 7 clear through our actual process. A momentary display change is not evidence that a repair or required check has been completed.
  15. Nia
    For scale A, we need more than a place to put ingredients. Its capacity and the required measurement performance have to fit the job.
  16. Omar
    Yes, it must be 8. I will not call it suitable just because it is another scale or appears to be free.
  17. Nia
    What should I tell pastry about the delay? I have no confirmed review or return time for B.
  18. Omar
    Do not promise a 9 time. We have a report to route and an alternative to verify, not a completed equipment decision.
  19. Nia
    My update to the team will be scale B unavailable for our task, use stopped, cause unknown, and scale A still awaiting your confirmation.
  20. Omar
    That accurately states the 10. I will handle the review route and alternative check, and communicate the actual outcome before anyone treats the issue as resolved.

Answers and explanations
  1. fault report Turn 2. The fault report identifies the specific equipment, observed problem, and action taken without claiming a diagnosed cause.
  2. observed symptom Turn 4. The observed symptom is the blank display; a cause or repair diagnosis requires appropriate assessment not supplied here.
  3. service request Turn 6. The service request routes the equipment problem for authorized review; submitting it is not the same as completing a repair.
  4. Equipment availability Turn 8. Equipment availability means the device can actually be assigned when needed; knowing scale A exists does not establish that.
  5. equipment identifier Turn 10. The equipment identifier distinguishes scale B from scale A or other devices, keeping the report tied to the correct asset.
  6. measurement result Turn 12. A measurement result concerns an actual recorded value; the display symptom alone does not determine every earlier reading's accuracy.
  7. out-of-use status Turn 14. Out-of-use status preserves the stopped-use condition until the actual review and authorization process determines the next action.
  8. fit for task Turn 16. Fit for task means meeting the actual weighing requirements, not merely being a different device that can display a number.
  9. return to use Turn 18. Return to use requires the relevant actual assessment and authorization; no timing or completed decision is supplied here.
  10. review status Turn 20. Review status distinguishes the current unresolved equipment question from a repaired scale or confirmed replacement.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nia: Omar, I need help with scale B at the pastry prep bench. Its display keeps going blank, so I have stopped using it for the weighing task.
  2. Omar: Thank you for making the fault report specific. Scale B, pastry prep bench, repeated blank display, and use stopped. Do you know the cause, or only the symptom?
  3. Nia: Only the display problem. It has happened repeatedly. I have stopped using B and have not opened it or tried a repair.
  4. Omar: Keep that as an observed symptom. We should not write that a particular component failed when we have not established it through the appropriate assessment.
  5. Nia: Can you arrange the equipment review and check whether scale A is available? I need a confirmed way forward for the work at this bench.
  6. Omar: I will arrange the appropriate service request. I also need to check scale A rather than assume it can be assigned to you immediately.
  7. Nia: I saw scale A earlier, but another station may need it. Could you check that as well as whether it fits this weighing task?
  8. Omar: Exactly. Equipment availability is one question, and suitability is another. We need both answered before presenting it as your confirmed alternative.
  9. Nia: Please keep the location in the report. A message saying the scale is blank could send someone to a different bench or device.
  10. Omar: I will include the equipment identifier and the pastry location. That gives the person reviewing it a clear starting point without a guessed cause.
  11. Nia: Does this automatically mean that everything I weighed earlier was wrong? I do not have evidence about those earlier readings from this symptom alone.
  12. Omar: It does not establish a specific measurement result as wrong. Any question about earlier work needs its own appropriate review, not a blanket conclusion or a blanket reassurance.
  13. Nia: I will not resume using scale B just because the display briefly comes back. The repeated blanking is the problem I am reporting.
  14. Omar: Keep the out-of-use status clear through our actual process. A momentary display change is not evidence that a repair or required check has been completed.
  15. Nia: For scale A, we need more than a place to put ingredients. Its capacity and the required measurement performance have to fit the job.
  16. Omar: Yes, it must be fit for task. I will not call it suitable just because it is another scale or appears to be free.
  17. Nia: What should I tell pastry about the delay? I have no confirmed review or return time for B.
  18. Omar: Do not promise a return to use time. We have a report to route and an alternative to verify, not a completed equipment decision.
  19. Nia: My update to the team will be scale B unavailable for our task, use stopped, cause unknown, and scale A still awaiting your confirmation.
  20. Omar: That accurately states the review status. I will handle the review route and alternative check, and communicate the actual outcome before anyone treats the issue as resolved.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A blank display is the observation

The scale problem from the case.

  1. Cook: I'm reporting an equipment fault with scale B at pastry prep: its display keeps going blank.
  2. Lead cook: Do you know the cause, or are you reporting only what the display did?
  3. Cook: Only what I observed. I don't know whether the issue is with power or the scale itself.
  4. Lead cook: I'll mark scale B out of service for review. Can you show me its label?
  5. Cook: Could you also confirm whether scale A is available so this prep can continue?
  6. Lead cook: I'll check its availability. Has the pastry quantity already been verified by another method?
  7. Cook: No, that quantity remains unverified. I haven't reported this part of the prep as complete.
  8. Lead cook: Then keep that status clear. I'll bring you a confirmed equipment option after checking.
  9. Cook: Should I give the next cook the device label and location in the handover?
  10. Lead cook: Yes, identify scale B at pastry prep, with review pending and no repair time confirmed.
A specific request for the dish team

A cook requests needed plates during a busy service.

  1. Cook: Could you check the availability of small soup bowls? Our station has four left.
  2. Dish colleague: One dish rack of small bowls is in progress, but I don't have a completion time.
  3. Cook: The bowls are the bottleneck now: six soup orders are pending, and we only have four.
  4. Dish colleague: Are you asking for all the bowls from that rack or just the immediate shortfall?
  5. Cook: The immediate shortfall is two bowls. The lead can decide how to allocate the rest.
  6. Dish colleague: I'll tell the lead that number and update you when the rack is ready.
  7. Cook: Please identify small soup bowls, not the larger pasta bowls stored beside them.
  8. Dish colleague: Understood. Is there a completed rack elsewhere that you've already checked for this size?
  9. Cook: I checked our station shelf only. I haven't checked the other approved storage location.
  10. Dish colleague: I'll confirm that location with the lead so we don't duplicate the same search.
A noisy machine needs a location

A kitchen assistant clarifies an equipment report.

  1. Assistant: There's an unusual noise from the dish area, but I haven't identified which machine is involved.
  2. Supervisor: Are you reporting the sound itself, or did you see a particular machine malfunction?
  3. Assistant: The sound only. I don't want to label the dishwasher faulty without checking with the operator.
  4. Supervisor: Good distinction. I'll speak with the dish lead and determine the appropriate response.
  5. Assistant: Should I tell the kitchen all dish service has stopped because of the noise?
  6. Supervisor: No, we haven't established that. Report the concern and keep any service impact unconfirmed.
  7. Assistant: I'll say the dish lead is reviewing an unusual noise, without naming a failed component.
  8. Supervisor: Please also tell me when you first noticed it so the report has a clear reference.
  9. Assistant: I noticed it just after eight, while collecting clean trays near the entrance.
  10. Supervisor: Thank you. That location and time are useful; a guessed diagnosis wouldn't improve the report.

Two scenarios to practice

Scale B keeps going blank
  1. Check the answers. Read the scale report with a partner, naming scale B and the pastry prep bench.
  2. Switch roles. Separate the observed blank display from a diagnosed cause; keep scale A's availability and suitability unconfirmed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report another display problem

Scale C at the cold-prep bench repeatedly goes blank. Cook Noor has stopped using it. The cause is unknown. Lead Maya will arrange review and verify whether scale D is available and suitable.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cook: "The affected scale is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. C

Scale C is the device with the reported display problem.

2. Lead: "Its location is the ___ bench."

Answer and explanation

A. cold-prep

The brief identifies the cold-preparation bench as the affected location.

3. Cook: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unknown

No cause has been established by the observed display behavior.

4. Lead: "The proposed alternative is scale ___."

Answer and explanation

B. D

Scale D still needs availability and suitability confirmation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 07: Reporting equipment and dish-station needs
Original fictional case: At the pastry prep bench, scale B repeatedly shows a blank display. Cook Nia has stopped using it. No cause, repair, or measurement error has been established. Lead Omar needs to arrange the appropriate review and confirm whether scale A is available and suitable for the task. Nia should name the equipment and location, describe the observed display behavior, and keep both review and alternative status clear. The case supplies no permission to repair scale B, no proof that previous batches are defective, and no confirmed time for returning it to use.
Language workshop: Scale B keeps going blank
Communication goal: Report an observable equipment problem, identify the affected work, and request review and a verified alternative.
Language guidance: The display keeps going blank is an observation. The power supply has failed is a cause claim not supplied by this case. Describe the symptom without opening, adjusting, or repairing equipment outside the actual authorized process.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Scale B at the pastry prep bench is affected. / Its display keeps going blank. / I have stopped using it. / I do not know the cause. / The weighing task cannot continue on this scale as normal. / Please arrange the appropriate equipment review. / Can you confirm whether scale A is available and suitable? / No repair has been completed. / Scale B has not been cleared for return to use. / We have not established an error in earlier batches. / The report concerns the pastry bench, not an unspecified station. / I will not attempt an unauthorized repair. / Please check scale A before we move the weighing task. / Who will receive the service request? / No return-to-use time has been confirmed. / Scale B, pastry bench, repeated blank display, use stopped, review needed.
Vocabulary: equipment identifier: Name or code distinguishing a particular piece of equipment. / asset tag: Label used to identify equipment within an organization's records. / fault report: Account of an equipment problem and its relevant context. / display: Screen or indicator presenting a device's readings or status. / intermittent fault: Problem that appears repeatedly but not necessarily continuously. / observed symptom: Behavior directly noticed before its cause is established. / diagnosis: Established explanation of a fault after appropriate assessment. / out-of-use status: State indicating equipment has been removed from use under the relevant process. / pastry bench: Work surface or station used for pastry preparation. / weighing task: Work requiring measurement of ingredient mass. / measurement result: Value produced by a measurement process. / measurement uncertainty: Quantified spread associated with a measurement result, not a known measurement error. / resolution: Smallest displayed or distinguishable increment of a measuring device. / capacity: Maximum load or operating range specified for the equipment. / calibration: Comparison against a reference to establish measurement relationships, not necessarily adjustment. / verification: Check that specified requirements are met. / adjustment: Intervention changing equipment response to meet a required condition. / service request: Request for authorized equipment assessment or maintenance. / authorized technician: Person permitted and qualified under the relevant arrangement to assess or service equipment. / alternative equipment: Different device proposed for the required task. / equipment availability: Whether a device can actually be assigned for use when needed. / fit for task: Suitable for the specific intended work under applicable requirements. / return to use: Resumption of operation after the required assessment and authorization. / review status: Current stage of the assessment, distinct from a completed repair.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the scale report with a partner, naming scale B and the pastry prep bench. Switch roles. Separate the observed blank display from a diagnosed cause; keep scale A's availability and suitability unconfirmed. Complete and check the scale C transfer. Repeat the cold-prep location and proposed scale D without changing their statuses.
Partner: Nia: Cook at the pastry prep bench / Omar: Kitchen lead receiving the equipment report
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Scale C at the cold-prep bench repeatedly goes blank. Cook Noor has stopped using it. The cause is unknown. Lead Maya will arrange review and verify whether scale D is available and suitable.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nia: What should I tell pastry about the delay? I have no confirmed review or return time for B.
Omar: Do not promise a return to use time. We have a report to route and an alternative to verify, not a completed equipment decision.
Nia: My update to the team will be scale B unavailable for our task, use stopped, cause unknown, and scale A still awaiting your confirmation.
Omar: That accurately states the review status. I will handle the review route and alternative check, and communicate the actual outcome before anyone treats the issue as resolved.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
