Dialogue 02: A substitute arrives without a clear message
Original fictional setting: A cook and receiving lead compare an actual delivery with the planned ingredient order.
Professional roles: Cook / Receiving lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Cook: The supplier sent diced peppers, but the order lists whole peppers. The case counts happen to match.
Receiving lead: Did the delivery note identify that as a substitution, or does it still name the original product?
Cook: It still says whole peppers. The driver called it an equivalent item, but there's no written change.
Receiving lead: Then matching case numbers doesn't settle the product difference. I'll contact the supplier about the incorrect description.
Cook: Should I tell the prep team the ingredient is available, or would that hide the unresolved substitution?
Receiving lead: Tell them a different product arrived and is awaiting review. Don't describe it as the ingredient they requested.
Cook: Their prep list depends on a specified product. I don't know whether this one suits the planned recipe.
Receiving lead: That's a decision for the kitchen lead after reviewing the actual product information, not just the supplier's description.
Cook: I'll give the lead both product names and the quantity received so they can assess the difference.
Receiving lead: Please also say the delivery record still names whole peppers; that record will need an accurate resolution.
Cook: Understood. The stock count is five cases, but product acceptance and the record correction are both unresolved.
Receiving lead: I'll ask the supplier; the lead will review product suitability.
Vocabulary in this exchange: prep list: A list of ingredients or components to prepare before or during service. / delivery note: A supplier's document listing items and quantities sent with a delivery. / substitution: A replacement ingredient or item requested instead of the standard one.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Dialogue 03: Two voices call the same correction
Original fictional setting: A cook checks whether duplicate messages request one remake or two.
Professional roles: Cook / Expediter
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Cook: You and the server both called a replacement soup for table ten. Is that one request repeated?
Expediter: Yes, one replacement only. The server spoke first, and I repeated it without saying it was the same.
Cook: I'll count one, then. Which ticket should carry the remake so another cook doesn't start a duplicate?
Expediter: Use ticket eighty-two. The replacement is for the diner at seat three, not the whole table.
Cook: The ticket also shows two original soups. Are either of those still waiting to be prepared?
Expediter: No, both originals were served. Only this single replacement remains open for the soup station now.
Cook: Thanks. I'll separate completed original orders from the new replacement when I report the outstanding quantity.
Expediter: Please also tell the server I've accepted their request, so they don't call it through again.
Cook: I can confirm acceptance, but the wait estimate still needs checking against the station's current work.
Expediter: That's fine. I'll explain that distinction to the server and ask them to wait for your estimate.
Cook: Then we have one outstanding replacement on eighty-two, seat three, and no additional original soups pending.
Expediter: Exactly. That shared count should stop a repeated message turning into a second production request.
Vocabulary in this exchange: ticket: A kitchen's written or electronic record of a food order.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Follow current local requirements, approved recipes, allergen controls, equipment instructions, and emergency procedures. No example authorizes serving unverified food, bypassing a control, or repairing faulty equipment.
Dialogue 04: An estimate belongs to a particular ticket
Original fictional setting: A busy cook and expediter untangle two timing updates.
Professional roles: Expediter / Cook
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Expediter: You said five minutes, but I didn't catch the ticket number. Was that for thirty or thirteen?
Cook: Five minutes is my estimate for thirty. Thirteen has its mains at the pass for checking.
Expediter: Thanks. The server for thirteen heard that number and thought their order had another five-minute delay.
Cook: Please correct that with them. My update concerned thirty's unfinished dishes, not the completed mains for thirteen.
Expediter: I'll check thirteen before calling its pickup. Does thirty's estimate include both the main and its side?
Cook: It includes the main only. The side station hasn't given me a time for their component yet.
Expediter: Then I can't describe the whole of thirty as five minutes away. I'll get the side-station update.
Cook: Exactly. Tell them the main estimate, so they can discuss the full table's timing with you.
Expediter: The side station estimates six minutes. I'll coordinate the pickup around both components rather than the earlier number.
Cook: Please call the full ticket number when you update the server; the similar numbers caused the first confusion.
Expediter: I will. Thirteen is being checked at the pass; thirty's full pickup is currently estimated at six minutes.
Cook: That matches our latest information. I'll report any change directly.
Vocabulary in this exchange: ticket: A kitchen's written or electronic record of a food order. / pass: The kitchen area where completed dishes are checked and collected for service. / pickup: The collection of dishes at the pass for delivery to a table or customer.
END REFERENCE

Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English

Dialogue 05: A substitution changes the description
Original fictional setting: A cook and server discuss a proposed alternative that would change the dish being offered.
Professional roles: Server / Cook
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Server: We're out of the vegetable tart. Does the pasta alternative include the same side salad?
Cook: No, the pasta comes without that salad. Please explain the difference before the guest decides.
Server: They asked for a lighter meal, so I'll check whether pasta still appeals to them.
Cook: Please check the menu price with them too. It may affect which alternative they choose.
Server: I'll do that. Is the pasta definitely available, or am I offering something whose count needs checking?
Cook: We have four unallocated portions right now. I'll confirm the remaining count when you bring their choice back.
Server: Can you reserve one while I speak to them, or would that require the expediter's approval here?
Cook: Ask the expediter about this ticket's allocation. I haven't reserved a portion just by describing the current count.
Server: Understood. I'll say pasta is the proposed alternative, without promising a reservation or an identical accompaniment.
Cook: Please bring back the guest's choice before changing the ticket.
Server: I'll use the actual dish name on the revised ticket and identify which original order it replaces.
Cook: Then we can verify the accepted dish and its side details.
Vocabulary in this exchange: ticket: A kitchen's written or electronic record of a food order. / expediter: The person coordinating tickets, dish readiness, and pickups between kitchen and service staff.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Dialogue 06: A verbal amendment arrives after a ticket
Original fictional setting: A cook and server clarify a dietary concern added after the initial order.
Professional roles: Cook / Server
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Cook: I've received a note saying 'change the sauce' for ticket forty, but it doesn't give a reason.
Server: The diner asked about an ingredient after ordering. I should have explained the concern with the amendment.
Cook: What exactly did they say? I need their words before the lead can assess the requested change.
Server: They said they avoid mustard and asked whether this sauce contains it. They haven't explained why yet.
Cook: Please ask whether they're reporting an allergy or a preference, and keep their food order pending meanwhile.
Server: I'll ask directly. I won't assume the answer just because their first request sounded casual at the table.
Cook: The lead also needs the actual sauce name; this ticket includes two dishes with different sauce components.
Server: The dietary modification concerns the side-salad dressing, not the sauce with the main.
Cook: Thank you. I'll identify the dressing and the unchanged main separately when I pass the question to the lead.
Server: I'll tell the diner which component we're checking and ask for their stated concern without suggesting an answer.
Cook: Then we can review the current dressing information before agreeing that a substitution addresses what they need.
Server: Yes. I'll get their answer before we suggest a replacement.
Vocabulary in this exchange: ticket: A kitchen's written or electronic record of a food order. / substitution: A replacement ingredient or item requested instead of the standard one. / pass: The kitchen area where completed dishes are checked and collected for service. / dietary modification: A requested change to a dish related to a diner's stated dietary need or choice.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: A missing utensil changes the station update
Original fictional setting: A cook and lead coordinate work when a needed utensil cannot be located.
Professional roles: Cook / Lead cook
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Cook: Our serving utensils aren't all back from the dish area. Should I call the station ready?
Lead cook: Tell me which item is missing and what remains incomplete, rather than using a general readiness label.
Cook: The small serving ladle is missing. The ingredient count and the rest of the setup are complete.
Lead cook: Have you asked the dish lead whether it's still in their area or already returned elsewhere?
Cook: They confirmed it's not yet returned, but they haven't located it. I don't know where it is.
Lead cook: Then report setup partly complete, with that utensil outstanding. I'll check whether a suitable replacement is available.
Cook: Should I borrow the other station's ladle now? They appear to have an extra one beside their setup.
Lead cook: Don't assume it's spare. I'll ask their lead about its allocation before deciding whether it can move.
Cook: I'll leave their setup unchanged and give our expediter the specific reason our station isn't fully ready.
Lead cook: Good. The other station confirms its extra ladle is allocated, so we'll need a different replacement option.
Cook: I'll keep our utensil request open. The completed ingredient count doesn't need repeating when the replacement arrives.
Lead cook: Once we have a replacement, just update that outstanding item.
Vocabulary in this exchange: expediter: The person coordinating tickets, dish readiness, and pickups between kitchen and service staff.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Follow current local requirements, approved recipes, allergen controls, equipment instructions, and emergency procedures. No example authorizes serving unverified food, bypassing a control, or repairing faulty equipment.
Dialogue 07: A missing utensil changes the station update
Original fictional setting: A cook and lead coordinate work when a needed utensil cannot be located.
Professional roles: Cook / Lead cook
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Cook: Our serving utensils aren't all back from the dish area. Should I call the station ready?
Lead cook: Tell me which item is missing and what remains incomplete, rather than using a general readiness label.
Cook: The small serving ladle is missing. The ingredient count and the rest of the setup are complete.
Lead cook: Have you asked the dish lead whether it's still in their area or already returned elsewhere?
Cook: They confirmed it's not yet returned, but they haven't located it. I don't know where it is.
Lead cook: Then report setup partly complete, with that utensil outstanding. I'll check whether a suitable replacement is available.
Cook: Should I borrow the other station's ladle now? They appear to have an extra one beside their setup.
Lead cook: Don't assume it's spare. I'll ask their lead about its allocation before deciding whether it can move.
Cook: I'll leave their setup unchanged and give our expediter the specific reason our station isn't fully ready.
Lead cook: Good. The other station confirms its extra ladle is allocated, so we'll need a different replacement option.
Cook: I'll keep our utensil request open. The completed ingredient count doesn't need repeating when the replacement arrives.
Lead cook: Once we have a replacement, just update that outstanding item.
Vocabulary in this exchange: expediter: The person coordinating tickets, dish readiness, and pickups between kitchen and service staff.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: The next shift inherits a count difference

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English

Dialogue 08: The next shift inherits a count difference
Original fictional setting: Two cooks compare a spoken handover with a stock record before changing the next shift's plan.
Professional roles: Incoming cook / Outgoing cook
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Incoming cook: Your spoken update said fourteen portions, but the stock sheet says sixteen. Which count is current?
Outgoing cook: Fourteen was my physical count just before handover. The sheet may still show the earlier count.
Incoming cook: Do you know why two portions differ, or are you only reporting the numbers you can see?
Outgoing cook: I only know the figures differ. I haven't matched the change to orders or a waste entry.
Incoming cook: Then we should leave the reason unresolved. Who will reconcile the physical count with the records?
Outgoing cook: I'll bring the discrepancy to the lead. Could you use fourteen as the observed count in our discussion?
Incoming cook: Yes, as an observed count, not a final reconciled figure or confirmation that all portions are available.
Outgoing cook: That's an important distinction. The lead also needs to confirm any allocations before updating the service availability.
Incoming cook: I'll keep the next prep decision pending until that availability figure is clear. Can you stay for the review?
Outgoing cook: I can stay for the handover discussion and show the lead where the fourteen portions were counted.
Incoming cook: Then I'll tell the next station that the count is under review, with the difference and owner identified.
Outgoing cook: I'll retain this follow-up until the lead explicitly accepts responsibility.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Cooks and Kitchen Staff English
Professional audience: Line cooks, prep cooks, kitchen assistants, and dish-team colleagues
Scope: All kitchens, tickets, quantities, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not culinary certification, food-safety procedures, medical advice, or equipment repair. Follow current local requirements, approved recipes, allergen controls, equipment instructions, and emergency procedures. No example authorizes serving unverified food, bypassing a control, or repairing faulty equipment.
Lesson 01: Clarifying a prep list
Original fictional case: The prep list says "soup: 24" without a unit. Yesterday's sheet used portions, while a storeroom label uses containers. Prep cook Asha has not started a batch. Lead Mateo intended twenty-four portions from the current recipe, not twenty-four containers. Asha needs to ask about the unit, confirm the current recipe, and make the corrected instruction visible. The conversation supplies no container capacity, portion volume, ingredient weights, or recipe conversion factor. Those details must come from the actual approved recipe and production arrangements, not from guessing what the number means.
Language workshop: Twenty-four of what?
Communication goal: Clarify an ambiguous prep quantity, confirm the recipe basis, and read back the instruction before production starts.
Language guidance: A portion is not a container unless the actual arrangement explicitly makes them equivalent. The number twenty-four cannot be converted into liters, containers, or ingredient weights without the relevant recipe and capacity information.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The prep list says soup, twenty-four, but the unit is missing. / Do you mean twenty-four portions or twenty-four containers? / I mean twenty-four portions. / Use the current recipe. / Not twenty-four containers. / I have not started the batch yet. / What portion size and finished yield does this recipe use? / I will use the yield stated in the approved recipe. / Container size does not define the portion count by itself. / Let us add portions to the prep list. / Soup: twenty-four portions, using the current recipe. / Which approved recipe version applies to this task? / Yesterday's sheet does not settle today's missing unit. / Ingredient quantities must come from the actual recipe calculation. / I will check the corrected instruction before production starts. / We now agree on the item, quantity, unit, and recipe basis.
Vocabulary: prep list: Record of food-preparation tasks and required quantities. / mise en place: Ingredients, equipment, and organization prepared for the work ahead. / portion: Specified amount intended for one serving or defined use. / batch: Quantity produced together in one preparation cycle. / batch size: Amount to be produced in a particular batch. / recipe yield: Quantity or number of portions an approved recipe produces. / portion size: Specified amount allocated to each serving. / unit of measure: Stated basis such as grams, liters, containers, or portions. / container capacity: Amount a particular container can hold. / production quantity: Amount planned for preparation. / standardized recipe: Defined recipe specifying ingredients, method, and yield for consistent production. / current recipe: Recipe version established for the present task. / recipe version: Identified edition or revision of a recipe. / scaling factor: Ratio used to adjust a recipe from its original yield to a target yield. / ingredient weight: Mass of a recipe component. / volume: Space occupied by a substance, measured in a stated unit. / tare: Container weight excluded to determine the contents' net weight. / net weight: Contents' weight excluding packaging or container weight. / gross weight: Combined weight of contents and relevant container or packaging. / production sheet: Record of planned and actual kitchen output. / prep target: Required output for a preparation task. / read-back: Repetition of an instruction to confirm the details understood. / clarification: Question or explanation that resolves an ambiguity. / task start: Point at which the assigned preparation begins.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the prep-list conversation, stressing portions rather than containers. Switch roles. Repeat the twenty-four-portion instruction, current recipe, and no-batch-started status without inventing a volume. Complete and check the sauce transfer. Read the eighteen-portion instruction and keep the tub interpretation rejected.
Partner: Asha: Prep cook / Mateo: Kitchen lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A prep line says sauce: 18. Lead Noor clarifies eighteen portions from the current recipe, not eighteen tubs. No batch has started, and no portion volume is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Asha: I will add portions to the line now. Whoever takes over this prep should not have to ask the same question again.
Mateo: Agreed. Make the production quantity explicit. The corrected entry should preserve soup, twenty-four portions, and the current recipe basis without implying the task is complete.
Asha: My repeat is soup, twenty-four portions, current recipe. No batch started. I will check the recipe details before beginning the actual preparation.
Mateo: That read-back is correct. We have resolved the missing unit, and the next step is to prepare against the approved recipe and actual kitchen process.
END REFERENCE

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
